:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:31]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:

Observed 2026-09-17. =org_divide= timed out twice through MCP with the identical arguments; calling =claude-code-ide-org-divide= directly through =emacsclient= completed. Both timeouts persisted **nothing** — the safe failure mode =72463b68= was closed on, no phantom success — but the message is only "The operation timed out", so the only correct response is to go and read the file, which is what was done both times before retrying.

*What was ruled out, so nobody re-measures it.* =org-id-update-id-locations= 0.0 s over five files. =--id-index= 0.0 s. =org-update-statistics-cookies= across the whole 13,000-line buffer 0.2 s. =--id-find= 0.0 s. The function's return value *is* propertized — it embeds =org-get-heading= through =format= — but prints to 284 characters against 103 clean, far too small to stall anything. No prompt anywhere in the call graph, which would have hung a non-interactive call exactly like this.

*So the remaining suspects are all above our code*: Claude Code's MCP client and whatever timeout it enforces, the HTTP transport, and upstream's =claude-code-ide-mcp-server.el= / =claude-code-ide-make-tool=. Notably it is not the server generally — =org_capture=, =org_amend=, =org_slice_add_member=, =org_set_property= and =org_pending_updates= all answered normally in the same minutes.

*Unmeasured and worth measuring first:* the duration of the successful direct call. It was made under a 150 s alarm and the timing was lost in the reply, so "slower than the MCP timeout" is assumed rather than known — it may have been fast, which would move the fault squarely upstream. Measure that before anything else.

*Configuration note for whoever picks it up:* =.mcp.json= sets no timeout and the environment sets none, so the server runs on the client default. The =timeout: 10= values in =.claude/settings.json= are **hook** timeouts and unrelated. Claude Code exposes MCP timeouts through environment variables rather than =.mcp.json= keys; confirm the names and defaults against the docs rather than from memory. If the fault proves upstream it belongs with =9d6449c7=.

*Measured 2026-09-17: **0.013 s**, and it settles the attribution.* =claude-code-ide-org-divide= run in a batch Emacs against full-size copies of TODO.org and DONE.org, with a freshly built id index, on the same heading that timed out twice through MCP. Thirteen milliseconds — a fraction of a percent of any plausible tool timeout.

So the "slower than the MCP timeout" assumption recorded above is **false**, and with it the last suspect inside this repo. The operation is effectively instantaneous; whatever consumed the time lives in Claude Code's MCP client, the HTTP transport, or upstream's =claude-code-ide-mcp-server.el= / =claude-code-ide-make-tool=. This belongs with =9d6449c7=.

*What a report needs next, which this repo cannot produce:* server-side instrumentation — when the request arrived, when the handler was entered, when the reply was written. Without it there is no way to tell a slow handler from a slow transport from a client giving up early, and all three are consistent with what was observed.

*One free thing to do regardless:* the tool's return value is propertized, embedding =org-get-heading= through =format=. At 284 printed characters against 103 clean it cannot be the cause, but a handler that walks or serialises text properties is a plausible home for a cost that scales with something other than length, and =substring-no-properties= costs nothing.

*Method note, recorded because the first attempt did damage.* This measurement was originally taken against the live Emacs buffer, and that same session ran =(org-update-statistics-cookies t)= there as a "timing probe" — a whole-buffer mutation under =inhibit-read-only= — which rewrote a quoted title in body prose from =[1/1]= to =[0/0]= and left the buffer dirty (=bf00d36a=). A timing probe must not mutate, and must not run against a live buffer when a scratch copy will do. The figure above was taken on copies, in batch, touching nothing.
:END:

The same `org_divide` call timed out through MCP and completed through `emacsclient`, so the work is in the transport rather than the tool. MCP timeouts are client-side env vars (`MCP_TIMEOUT`, `MCP_TOOL_TIMEOUT`), not settings in `.mcp.json` — establish which limit fired and whether the wrapper we inherit from `claude-code-ide` imposes its own.

*Evidence 2026-09-17 that the premise may be wrong: at least some of these "timeouts" are not timeouts.* Two consecutive =org_set_todo= calls returned "The operation timed out" to the model and queued nothing. Emacs had in fact answered immediately, and the user relayed what it showed: =peculiar error: -32602, "Missing required argument: state"=. The call had passed =keyword=, not =state=.

So the server rejected the parameters, replied with a well-formed JSON-RPC error, and **the client surfaced that as a timeout**. The model retried verbatim, because a timeout is the one failure that invites a retry and a =-32602= is the one that never should — the second attempt could not have succeeded and the diagnosis was unreachable from this side.

Two consequences for this heading. The =emacsclient=-succeeds-where-MCP-times-out observation is consistent with a parameter mismatch rather than a transport limit, since a hand-written =emacsclient= call passes the elisp arguments directly and never crosses the schema. And the thing worth fixing may not be a timeout value at all: **an error response that reads as a timeout is the defect**, and it is the same silent-failure class as =:ID:= 4f9e552a — a failure indistinguishable from a different, more forgiving one. Establish which layer swallows the error body before touching =MCP_TIMEOUT=.


* TODO [0/2] Make the corpus searchable by tag, by measurement where it can be and by decision where it cannot
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ee3e0fa2-d4a6-4575-a5fb-e4e25c49431a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:26]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:PLAN:

Divided out of =e28933e9= on 2026-09-17, which becomes the child and keeps its id, clock and history. The two halves want different schedules and that is the whole reason for the split: the **survey** is deliberately parked as expensive in time and tokens, while a tag adopted **by decision** is a same-day act. Holding the ready half hostage to the parked one is what =8bbae3aa= exists to stop one tier up, and the same argument divides this heading.

The division also names a real seam rather than a convenience. =e28933e9= derives a vocabulary *from* the corpus — "a measurement first and a taxonomy second", in its own words. Its sibling declares one *for* a purpose, with no measurement available to it: whether a defect was observed in use is a fact about how it was found, not a pattern latent in the text. A survey cannot recover it and should not try.

What they share, and why one parent: both answer "which tags make this corpus worth searching", and both land in the same two places — the =#+TAGS:= line and the org conventions' §Tags section. An adopted tag from either half constrains the other, so they should not drift apart in separate parts of the file.
:END:

The corpus answers "what did we decide" badly: a decision lives in one body and nothing indexes it. Two children carry the halves — tagging what can be measured, and tagging what can only be decided. Whether `org-agenda` or `org-ql` can build the view natively is an open question deferred until Emacs stops wedging.

** NEXT Adopt an `observed` tag for a defect seen in use, as the severity signal the keyword set does not carry :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8b945c02-7ebe-40a2-b4cc-e35811afe57f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:30]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-17 Thu 11:30] \\
  the cheap half; the survey stays parked
:END:
:PLAN:

*The observation, 2026-09-17.* The user noted an implicit severity range among open TODOs, by impact on usability and correctness. Measured against the corpus's own declared priority mechanism — "priority is expressed through keyword choice" — there is none: time-tracking work sits at =MAYBE= 9.1% against 8.2% for everything else, and both are 80% plain =TODO=. The keyword is not ranking anything, because four headings in five hold the same one.

*The recommendation: one tag, adopted by decision rather than derived by survey.* =:observed:= marks a defect **seen in use** rather than filed speculatively. The query it exists to answer — which this parent's sibling requires of any proposed tag — is *what is likely to break a consumer*, which is the question every slice composition has had to answer by re-reading the backlog.

*Why a tag and not a =:SEVERITY:= property.* This project's evidence is that a declared field nobody derives goes stale invisibly: the slice checkbox is derived from the referent's keyword for exactly this reason, =:DROPPED:= replaced bare cookie-deletion because absence was ambiguous, and a =:BLOCKER:= on a =MAYBE= reads as enforcement while enforcing nothing. A severity field over 128 open headings needs triage now and re-triage forever. =:observed:= needs neither: it is applied at filing when the knowledge is freshest, and "was seen in use" never changes.

*And why it does not wait on the survey.* The survey is deliberately parked as expensive; this is a same-day decision. Holding the cheap ready half hostage to the expensive unfinished one is what =8bbae3aa= exists to stop one tier up, and the same reasoning divides this heading from its sibling.

*Scope, if adopted:* add to =#+TAGS:= in both files and the templates, a line in the org conventions' §Tags saying what it means and when to apply it, and a backfill decision — probably **no** blanket backfill, since "observed" is a claim about how a defect was found and cannot be reconstructed for old headings without re-reading them, which is the survey's job anyway.
:END:

The keyword set carries no severity, so a defect someone actually hit reads the same as one reasoned about. An `:observed:` tag is the cheapest signal that distinguishes them, and it is a fact rather than a judgement — either it was seen in use or it was not.

Composed 2026-09-17 at the user's direction, whose framing is the point: task tracking, dependency management and slice planning are ready to support real work elsewhere, and time tracking is not. Shipping them together holds the useful half hostage to the half still under construction.

*The evidence is the session rather than the backlog.* Of 128 open headings, 55 sit in =Queue=, =Apply= and =Clock= — 43% — but weighting that by the corpus's own declared priority mechanism changes nothing: time-tracking work is deferred to =MAYBE= at 9.1% against 8.2% for everything else, and both sit at 80% plain =TODO=. The real asymmetry is behavioural. On 2026-09-17 every apply failure was a clock or span item, five of them; the user asked three separate times what a span represented; an eleven-hour overnight interval was offered as work, and a single keystroke would have recorded 3h11m over sixteen observed minutes. Against that, not one interruption came from capture, refile, blockers, slices, conventions or lint.

*What survives the cut, which is most of the plugin:* capture with =:CATEGORY:= enforcement, refile, divide, query, outline, the slice machinery with derived cookies and blockers, the =:PLAN:=/=:DEBRIEF:= drawers, the conventions as promoted rules, =lint-org=, =check-conventions=, and the footnote hook. The queue and the review pass survive too — =org_set_todo= is queued because org's state-change logging needs a genuinely interactive command, which has nothing to do with time.

*The shape decided before composing.* Scripts ship, wiring does not: a hook has no "disabled" state, so wired and unwired are the only forms available, and shipping unwired keeps the opt-in to one file edit. Not two plugins — time tracking is a **layer** on task tracking rather than a peer, since spans attribute to headings the task half manages and both share one queue, one review buffer and one watermark file. That decision carries one unmeasured assumption, which is a member here rather than a footnote.

*This slice does not fix time tracking and does not pretend to.* The 55 open items stay open and stay this repo's work. What changes is that they stop blocking =6a207b00=.

Planned:

- [X] [[id:eb6c4a9b-8f78-42fa-9e86-484bf352fcf6][eb6c4a9b]] DONE clock-target-check ships wired and enforces clocking, so it would block every turn once clocking is off
- [X] [[id:0c5577dc-ef15-440a-bdac-b3cb068aa7b2][0c5577dc]] DONE Take the guidepost, block and clock rows out of the shipped wiring, leaving every script on disk
- [X] [[id:36952d1f-5312-4cf2-a710-4290ce673e19][36952d1f]] DONE The promoted rules instruct clocking unconditionally, so a consumer is told to call tools that do nothing
- [ ] [[id:01f7a19c-4b71-447c-8ded-385410e5d607][01f7a19c]] TODO Decide whether org_clock_in and org_clock_out stay registered when nothing consumes what they queue
- [ ] [[id:6956a46e-da4b-44c2-a701-80196a2930c1][6956a46e]] TODO The SessionStart report mixes stale-interval recovery with the ceremony prompt, and only one half survives
- [X] [[id:1b36c5bd-bafd-4c43-8aae-c94dee45c5eb][1b36c5bd]] DONE claude-org-setup gains an opt-in that restores the time-tracking wiring and its rules
- [ ] [[id:d3149a81-91d2-4722-b6d8-68d4cee6fa4f][d3149a81]] TODO Verify the review buffer and apply pass on a queue that has never held a guidepost
- [X] [[id:5fb70821-0f46-42d9-8bf3-93bc98025692][5fb70821]] DONE Establish whether two plugin manifests can share one MCP server, which is what decides the two-plugin question
- [-] [[id:1254c24b-0dda-436e-a3d4-213de283692f][1254c24b]] DOING The prose still promises time tracking: README, CLAUDE.md, DOOM.md and the skill all describe a clock that no longer ships

*Decided by the user 2026-09-17, and it is larger than this slice: **time tracking is no longer a co-equal goal**.* "We have prioritized the other goal by committing to providing the ability to disable the time-tracking features." Committing to a switch **is** the ordering — a feature you can turn off is not co-equal with the one you cannot.

*That contradicts both always-loaded statements of what this project is for.* =README.md= opens "It has a second, co-equal goal: **trustworthy tracking of where attention actually went**… Much of the machinery here — the event queue, the session hooks, the review pass — exists for that goal rather than the first one." =CLAUDE.md= says the same at line 9, and is loaded into **every session by default**, which makes it the more consequential of the two: it is what each future session believes the project is for before reading anything else.

*So the correction is not a wording pass, and =1254c24b= should not treat it as one.* The demotion needs saying once, plainly, in both files: natural-language org manipulation is the goal; attention tracking is a second, optional capability, developed here and shipped off. Everything downstream — the feature/state/toggle description, the rules, the skill — follows from that sentence and should not re-argue it.

*One nuance worth preserving rather than flattening.* The =README= claim that "much of the machinery — the event queue, the session hooks, the review pass — exists for that goal" is **historically true** and stays true: the queue was built because concurrent sessions writing live clock state produced a run of desync bugs. What changes is the *ranking*, not the history. A demotion that erased why the machinery looks as it does would cost a future reader the only explanation on record.

*And it sharpens this slice's own justification.* The measured case was behavioural — every failure and every unanswerable question of 2026-09-17 came from a span or a clock item. The ordering decision is the same conclusion reached from the other direction, by a human weighing goals rather than counting incidents.

*The shape decision's premise is false, found 2026-09-18 while researching =1b36c5bd=.* The slice was composed on "a hook has no 'disabled' state, so wired and unwired are the only forms available". **A third form exists.** Claude Code plugins declare user-configurable options in =plugin.json='s =userConfig=, with =type: boolean= and a =default=; the values reach hook processes as =CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_<KEY>=. A hook can therefore be *wired but self-gating* — one line at the top of each script, exiting 0 when the option is off. Confirmed against the docs and against this machine's Claude Code **2.1.272**, past the 2.1.271 the option picker requires.

What that would look like: =userConfig.time_tracking= as a boolean defaulting =false=; the six time scripts each open with a guard on =$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_TIME_TRACKING=; =hooks/hooks.json= keeps every row it had. The consumer turns it on in =/config=, or at install with =claude plugin install ... --config time_tracking=true=.

*Why this is better than what the slice chose, on the slice's own terms.* It removes =1b36c5bd='s whole problem rather than solving it — no flag has to write hook wiring anywhere, so the absolute-path objection and the =${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}=-does-not-expand-in-settings.json finding both become moot. The toggle is discoverable where a consumer already looks, and flipping it edits no JSON by hand. The per-event cost is one env-var test and an exit, which is the shape =block-end= was already written to have.

*What it costs, stated plainly:* it **reopens =eb6c4a9b= and =0c5577dc=**, both closed today on the removal decision. Under self-gating their rows go back into =hooks/hooks.json= and each script gains a guard — so the work is not discarded (the inventory, the row-level verification and the =clock-target-check= reasoning all still apply) but the commit is substantially rewritten. It also makes the plugin depend on a Claude Code version supporting =userConfig=.

*And one thing it does not solve:* rules promotion. =claude-org-setup= cannot see a plugin option, so =org-time-tracking.md= would stay promoted unconditionally — which is where it already is, and its opening already tells the reader not to infer the feature is installed from its presence. Under self-gating that preamble should point at the option rather than at a =session-pause= row.

Not acted on: this is the user's call, and it reverses a composed decision mid-slice.


* TODO Verify the review buffer and apply pass on a queue that has never held a guidepost :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d3149a81-91d2-4722-b6d8-68d4cee6fa4f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:16]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:PLAN:

The claim this slice rests on: with no guideposts and no clock events, the review buffer shows only state changes, captures and amends — the item classes that have applied cleanly throughout, while every failure and every unanswerable question of 2026-09-17 came from a span or a clock item.

It is a claim, not a fact, and the corpus cannot test it: this repo's queue has held guideposts since the 2026-08-11 cutover, so every existing test and every manual pass has run against a queue that has them. Wanted is a fixture whose queue contains only =todo=, =capture= and =amend= events, asserting that =--review-items-from-queue= yields exactly those items, that the buffer renders and applies them, and that nothing in the span or complement path is reached.

Two failure modes to probe rather than assume. =--queue-drained-p= is "yields no items", and archiving depends on it — a queue that drains differently may change when archiving fires. And =apply-detect= compares watermark mtimes; with fewer event kinds moving them, its "the record is fresh" message may fire less often or not at all.
:END:

Every review pass this project has run has read a queue full of guideposts. With the hooks cut, a consumer's queue holds captures, amends and state changes and nothing else — a shape the review buffer and apply pass have never been exercised against. Verify before shipping, not after a consumer reports it.


* DONE Establish whether two plugin manifests can share one MCP server, which is what decides the two-plugin question :research:
CLOSED: [2026-09-18 Fri 12:32]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5fb70821-0f46-42d9-8bf3-93bc98025692
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:16]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:DEBRIEF:
*Answered by reading this repo's own packaging rather than by experiment, 2026-09-18.* No second plugin was built; everything below is read off files that exist.

*Why the question dissolves.* =.claude-plugin/plugin.json= declares ="mcpServers": "./.mcp.json"=, and that file declares =emacs-tools= as ={"type": "http", "url": "http://localhost:45571/mcp/warp"}=. The server is **not a process a plugin spawns** — it is the running Emacs, at a well-known URL. "Sharing" a stdio server would be a question about process ownership; sharing an HTTP endpoint is not a question at all, because any number of clients may point at one URL.

And the case we actually care about is thinner still: a time plugin would declare **no MCP server whatsoever**. =org_clock_in= and =org_clock_out= are registered by =config.el= and served by the same endpoint the task plugin already points at, so the second plugin's whole content would be =hooks/hooks.json= plus =org-time-tracking.md=. Nothing to share.

*The obstacle that replaces it, and it is the reason the answer is still "no".* The shipped wiring reaches its scripts through =${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}=, which resolves to *the declaring plugin's* root. A time plugin's rows would therefore point into the time plugin — where the scripts are not. And they cannot simply be referenced across, because =bin/hooks/session-pause= ends:

: exec "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")/queue-append" pause

**Sibling resolution.** So a time plugin must ship its own =queue-append= beside its own =session-pause=. Measured: =session-pause= 22 lines, =session-resume= 37, =block-start= 62, =block-end= 67, =clock-target-check= 143, and =queue-append= **244** — and that last one is the file the *task* plugin also needs, for its =todo=/=capture=/=amend= rows. Two plugins means two copies of one 244-line script, free to drift, with no mechanism that would notice.

*One thing that is genuinely fine, and worth recording so it is not raised as an objection later.* The queue directory is =${CLAUDE_ORG_QUEUE_DIR:-$HOME/.claude/org-updates}= — **global, not per-plugin** — so two copies of =queue-append= would still append to one queue and one review buffer. There is no split-brain risk. The cost is duplication, not divergence of the record.

*So the original decision holds, and its weakest leg is now measured rather than assumed.* The composition heading rejected two plugins partly because the second "would ship no code of its own". That understated it: the second would ship **duplicated** code, which is worse than none.

*Two claims below the evidentiary bar, flagged rather than asserted.* That a =PostToolUse= matcher in plugin B fires for a tool served by plugin A's MCP server is *expected* — the matcher matches the tool name =mcp__emacs-tools__org_clock_in=, which is a property of the server rather than of the declaring plugin — but it was reasoned, not observed. And whether =${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}= expands in a *project's* own =.claude/settings.json= (as opposed to a plugin's =hooks.json=) is still unverified; it is the question that now decides =1b36c5bd='s shape, and it is recorded there.

*The revisit trigger is unchanged and still narrow:* a consumer wanting the time half **without** the task half. That is incoherent while spans attribute to org headings the task half manages, and would become coherent only if they ever attributed to something else.

*Sequencing note, because the record should show the plan file was wrong and the heading was right.* =next-session.md= placed this member last, saying it "decides nothing this slice does, only whether the two-plugin question reopens later". This heading's own body said the opposite — "Answer the mechanical question first — it decides the packaging, and the packaging decides how much of the rest of this slice is even possible" — and its =NEXT= nomination note said it "sequences first". It was promoted ahead of =1b36c5bd= on 2026-09-18 after that member's own assumption collapsed, which rediscovered the same ordering independently.
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-17 Thu 16:50] \\
  answers the assumption the slice's not-two-plugins decision rests on, so it sequences first
CLOCK: [2026-09-18 Fri 12:30]--[2026-09-18 Fri 12:32] =>  0:02
- State "DOING"      from "NEXT"       [2026-09-18 Fri 12:30] \\
  promoted ahead of 1b36c5bd: --time's shape depends on this, exactly as this heading's own body predicted
- [2026-09-18 Fri 12:30]--[2026-09-18 Fri 12:32]
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-09-18 Fri 12:32] \\
  not two plugins, measured: the sharing question dissolves, but a thin plugin must duplicate the 244-line queue-append
:END:
:PLAN:

The user asked 2026-09-17 whether this is becoming two plugins. The recommendation was **not yet**, on three grounds — but one of them is an assumption that should be measured rather than carried.

*The two settled grounds.* Time tracking is not a peer of task tracking, it is a **layer on it**: spans attribute to headings the task half manages, and both share one queue, one review buffer and one watermark file, so two peer plugins would misrepresent the dependency. And a second plugin would ship no code of its own — every tool is registered by one 17,000-line =config.el= that both would need — so its entire content would be hook wiring and one rules file.

*The open one.* Whether Claude Code supports two manifests sharing a single MCP server at all. This repo has exactly one =.claude-plugin/plugin.json= and no nested manifests, so the mechanics are untested here. If two manifests *can* share a server, the thin second plugin becomes viable and the "ships no code" objection weakens considerably — the wiring would be its code.

Answer it before the question is asked again, and record the answer either way. The trigger for revisiting the whole decision is narrower and worth writing down now: a consumer wanting the time half **without** the task half, which is incoherent today and would stop being so only if spans ever attributed to something other than org headings.
:END:

Splitting the tracker from the clock is only worth doing as two plugins if two manifests can share one MCP server registration; if they cannot, the split has to stay a flag on one plugin. Answer the mechanical question first — it decides the packaging, and the packaging decides how much of the rest of this slice is even possible.

*Answered 2026-09-18: two manifests never need to share an MCP server, because a time plugin would declare none.* The tools are served over HTTP by the running Emacs at a fixed URL, so a second plugin shipping only hooks and a rules file reaches them through the first plugin's registration. The question as posed has no force — but a different obstacle takes its place, and it is decisive: =${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}= would resolve to the *second* plugin's root, and =session-pause= resolves =queue-append= as a **sibling**, so the thin plugin must carry its own copy of all six scripts including the 244-line shared one. Recommendation unchanged, now measured: **not two plugins.**

  researching the opt-in mechanism; userConfig may replace the flag entirely

CLOCK: [2026-09-18 Fri 13:56]--[2026-09-18 Fri 14:07] =>  0:11
- [2026-09-18 Fri 13:56]--[2026-09-18 Fri 14:07]
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-09-18 Fri 14:07] \\
  shipped as a userConfig gate in c257553, not a setup flag; nothing writes wiring anywhere
:END:
:DEBRIEF:
*Shipped* in [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::c257553][c257553]]. The member's *goal* — "disabled-by-default is only defensible if turning it on is one documented command" — is met; its *mechanism* is not the one planned, and the route to that is the whole story.

*The plan's assumption collapsed on first contact.* "It adds the guidepost, block and clock rows to the consumer's wiring" presumes =claude-org-setup= writes hook wiring. It never has — it promotes rules, scaffolds org files, links the skill, writes Doom glue, and touches neither =settings.json= nor =hooks.json=. So =--time= was a new capability, not an extension, and its first question was *where does it write*.

*Every answer to that question was bad, and chasing them is what found the real one.* Into the consumer's =.claude/settings.json= needs an absolute path to the plugin — machine-specific, in a committed file, the objection the symlink code already records. Into =settings.local.json= answers that but makes the opt-in per-clone. A second plugin would let =${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}= resolve correctly, which is why =5fb70821= was promoted ahead of this member — and its answer was no, because the thin plugin would have to duplicate the 244-line =queue-append=. Confirmed from the docs along the way, having been flagged as an assumption: **=${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}= does not expand in a project's own =settings.json=**, only in a plugin's =hooks.json=.

*The answer was in the same documentation page as that confirmation.* Plugins declare =userConfig= options in =plugin.json= — =type: boolean=, with a =default= — and Claude Code exports them to hook processes as =CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_<KEY>=. So a hook can be **wired and self-gating**, which is the third state the slice was composed believing did not exist. Nothing writes wiring; the consumer flips a toggle in =/config=.

*What shipped.* =userConfig.time_tracking=, boolean, =default: false=. Three guards — =queue-append= kind-scoped (so =todo=/=capture=/=amend= stay unconditional), =block-start= (writes its sentinel directly), =clock-target-check= (blocks the =Stop=, never touches the queue). Four of the five time scripts route through =queue-append=, which is why three guards cover six scripts. =hooks/hooks.json= restored byte-identically to its pre-cut state.

*The default is the load-bearing choice.* Off, because the absent-variable case must fail safe: a consumer whose Claude Code predates =userConfig= sees no variable at all, and defaulting on would hand them guideposts they never asked for — the exact outcome the slice exists to prevent. The cost is the mirror image, an old client that cannot turn it *on*, which is the right direction to fail.

*And this repo needed an answer too.* It wires the same scripts through its own =.claude/settings.json=, where there is no plugin option to read, so its eight time rows set =CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_TIME_TRACKING=true= in the command string. **Wiring the row is the opt-in there** — which is why the two wirings stopped being mirrors, now stated correctly in =CLAUDE.md=.

*Verified, and made to discriminate rather than merely pass.* Against a temp queue directory: =clock_in= wrote 0 lines with the option unset, 0 with it =false=, 1 with it =true=; =todo= wrote with the option unset; =pause= did not. =block-start= created no sentinel when off and one when on; =clock-target-check= exited 0 when off. =claude plugin validate= accepts the manifest (one pre-existing warning about =CLAUDE.md= at the plugin root). =check-conventions= green, =bin/test= 688/688, rules re-promoted and in sync.

*One planned sub-goal is now moot, deliberately.* "=--check= should report the time-tracking wiring present or absent" cannot be done: the state lives in =~/.claude/settings.json= under =pluginConfigs=, which =claude-org-setup= has no business reading. =/config= is where a consumer sees it, and =org-time-tracking.md= says so.

*Cost, recorded honestly:* this reversed =eb6c4a9b= and =0c5577dc= hours after they closed. Both keep their analysis — the row inventory was reused verbatim, and the =block-end=-wired-twice finding still holds — and both carry the supersession in their own debriefs.
:END:
:PLAN:

If the scripts ship unwired, something has to make wiring them a single act rather than a hand edit of a JSON file the consumer did not write. =claude-org-setup= already promotes rules and scaffolds org files behind flags — =--doom=, =--org=, =--glue=, =--check= — so a =--time= flag is the established shape: it adds the guidepost, block and clock rows to the consumer's wiring and promotes =org-session-tracking.md= alongside them.

Two constraints it inherits. The repo-versus-plugin wiring trap is already documented — "a repo must enable only one of the two, or every guidepost is appended twice" — so the flag has to know which wiring the consumer is using before adding rows to either. And =--check= should report the time-tracking wiring as present or absent rather than silently ignoring it, since a half-applied opt-in is exactly the state that produces events nobody applies.

*Found 2026-09-18, before starting: the plan's central assumption does not hold.* "It adds the guidepost, block and clock rows to the consumer's wiring" presumes =claude-org-setup= writes hook wiring. **It never has.** It promotes rules, scaffolds org files, links the skill and writes Doom glue; grepped for =settings.json= and =hooks.json= and it touches neither. So =--time= is not an extension of an existing capability, it is a new one, and where it writes is an open question rather than a detail.

Three candidate shapes, and the difference is where the scripts' path comes from:

- *Into the consumer's =.claude/settings.json=.* This repo wires the same scripts that way — but with =${CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR}=, which works only because the scripts *are* in this project. A consumer's are in the plugin, so the rows would need an absolute path to the plugin root: machine-specific, in a version-controlled file, which is precisely the objection the symlink code already records ("a machine's path is not the project's to commit").
- *Into =.claude/settings.local.json=*, which is the conventional home for uncommitted machine-local settings. That answers the path objection and makes the opt-in per-machine rather than per-repo — arguably correct, since whose attention is being tracked is a property of the person, not the project. This is the cheapest shape and should be disproved before the third is considered.
- *A second plugin carrying the time hooks*, installed alongside. This is the only shape where =${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}= resolves correctly for the consumer without anyone writing a path — and it is blocked on =5fb70821=, whose whole question is whether two manifests can share one MCP server.

*Consequence for the slice's order:* =5fb70821= was about to be deferred as research that "decides nothing this slice does". That is no longer true — it decides between shape two and shape three here, so it moves ahead of this member rather than out of the slice.

*One thing to verify rather than assume before any of this:* whether =${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}= is expanded in a project's own =settings.json= hooks, or only in a plugin's =hooks.json=. If the former, shape one loses its path objection entirely and this gets much simpler.
:END:

The counterpart to the cut: a flag on `claude-org-setup` that puts the guidepost, block and clock rows back into the consuming repo's wiring and promotes the session-tracking reference alongside them. Disabled-by-default is only defensible if turning it on is one documented command.

*Resolved 2026-09-18 — the opt-in shipped, but not as a =claude-org-setup= flag, so this title names a mechanism that was replaced before it was built.* Time tracking is gated by a =time_tracking= boolean in the plugin's =userConfig=, read by the hook scripts themselves; nothing writes wiring anywhere, which is what dissolved every problem this member had accumulated.

The two queued clock tools are registered by the shared Doom module, so they remain callable whatever =hooks.json= wires. Three options, and the choice is about what an agent is *invited* to do rather than about correctness.

*Leave them registered.* Cheapest, and harmless in the narrow sense — they queue events and the review buffer shows clock items nobody has to apply. But a tool in the schema is an invitation, and this project's own evidence is that an agent calls what it is offered: the rules were followed exactly even when they produced fifteen-second brackets.

*Gate registration on a =defcustom=.* Honest — the surface then matches the feature set — at the cost of a conditional registration block and a second configuration to test.

*Remove them.* Not viable: this repo needs them, and they are the same module.

Note the tools are only half the invitation. =org_set_todo='s own description and the transition rules tell the agent when to call them, so gating registration without =36952d1f= leaves the instruction pointing at a tool that is gone — which is the louder failure of the two.
:END:

Separate from the hook wiring: the two MCP tools are registered by `config.el`, not by the manifest, so cutting the hooks leaves them callable. Decide whether an unconsumed queue event is harmless bookkeeping or a tool that should refuse, and say which.

:DEBRIEF:
*Shipped* in [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::373e60a][373e60a]] and [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::199ab6f][199ab6f]], split so the reviewable units match the risk: the first is a relocation with a new file, the second is per-passage surgery.

*The plan's premise was false, and measuring it first changed the work.* =next-session.md= said =org-session-tracking.md= is "*entirely* time tracking" and should simply not promote. It is three sections and the first is mixed: the =queue-append= matchers for =todo=/=capture=/=amend=, =footnote-check=, =apply-detect= and its whole paragraph, and the ceremony half of =SessionStart= are all task-tracking. These rules load in **every** session, so dropping the file would have taken task guidance away from a consumer — the opposite of the slice's point. Had the plan been followed as written the regression would have shipped.

*Fork over conditional, decided by the user against the project's own evidence.* A conditional clause is read as an instruction whatever its condition — the same bet as a path-scoped rule that does not load — and =01f7a19c='s argument that "an agent calls what it is offered" applies to prose as much as to a tool schema. The file-level split means a consumer without the feature never sees the instruction at all.

| always promoted | promoted with =--time= |
|---|---|
| =footnote-check=, =apply-detect=, the three =queue-append= matchers, the daily ceremony | guideposts, =:LOGBOOK:= CLOCK mechanics, permission blocks, the three numbers, stale-interval recovery |
| =DOING=/=REVIEW=/=WAITING= semantics, plural =DOING=, =NEXT= and nomination, the =@=-cookie hang, capture, =:CREATED:=, Plan Mode | the transition table's side-effect column, both to/from-=DOING= rules, the retroactive and grouping exceptions, the =org_clock_in=-before-first-write rule |

*How relocation was verified.* Every non-blank line of each original was tested for a verbatim match in the new pair, and the misses enumerated: five paragraphs in the first file and six in the second, each a deliberate rewrite with a named replacement. That is the conventions' own "relocation is lossless and verifiable" discipline, run as a check rather than asserted. One trap worth recording: =grep= treats a line beginning =-= as an option, so four bullets reported as missing until the scan was re-run with =-e= — a false alarm that looked exactly like real loss.

*The transition table now exists in both files on purpose.* =bin/check-conventions= parses it for the keyword set, so the transitions copy keeps all eight keywords and drops only the clock column. Removing it outright would have failed a check that exists to catch keyword drift.

*Accepted, not overlooked:* the new file promotes **unconditionally** until =1b36c5bd= lands =--time=. That is the status quo for a consumer rather than a regression, and the file's own opening says nothing in it applies with the default wiring. =CLAUDE.md='s pointer, which claimed the three numbers and stale-interval recovery live in =org-session-tracking.md=, was corrected in the same commit and now records that =hooks.json= and =.claude/settings.json= are deliberately no longer mirrors.

*Left for the members that own them, not filed as residue:* =skills/org/SKILL.md= instructs clocking in 48 places including its own transition table — that is =1254c24b='s surface, and its CLOCK-*parsing* sections should survive there, since a consumer with time tracking off still reads =.org= files whose drawers were written by hand. =org-mcp-tools.md= and =org-event-queue.md= still name the clock tools, which stays correct until =01f7a19c= decides whether they remain registered.

*Checks:* =claude-org-setup --check= in sync across 7 files, =check-conventions= green including "keyword set: org-state-transitions reference table" and "promoted rules match the plugin references", =check-org-dev-skill= green, =bin/test= 688/688.

*Correction to the paragraph above, same day.* "Six in the second" undercounts: the loss scan also flagged the side-effect preamble, the =REVIEW=-is-experimental paragraph and the "Two consequences … amnesty" block, none of which is among the six enumerated. **Nine, not six.** The scan was read and every miss accounted for at the time — what was inexact is this record of it, which is the same failure as a comment overstating a guard.

*And one defect this debrief did not catch, fixed in* [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::990fd10][990fd10]]: =org-time-tracking.md= opened by telling the reader that its own presence proved the wiring was installed. False for every consumer until =1b36c5bd=, since the file promotes unconditionally — it loads exactly where none of it applies. The "accepted, not overlooked" paragraph above covers the *promotion* and missed the *assertion*, which is the more dangerous half: a promotion gap is visible in =claude-org-setup=, a false sentence in an always-loaded rule is not.
:END:
:PLAN:

=org-state-transitions.md= is promoted into every consuming project and its transition table's whole right-hand column is clock instructions — "Open a CLOCK (call =org_clock_in=)" on four rows, "Close the CLOCK" on four more — plus the standing rule to clock in before a session's first write. =org-session-tracking.md= is promoted too and is *entirely* about time tracking: guideposts, the three numbers, permission blocks, stale-interval recovery.

With clocking off these tell a consuming agent to make calls that queue events nobody will ever apply, and to follow a rule whose backstop has been removed. Worse than useless: the rules are the thing an agent is *most* likely to obey exactly.

Two shapes to weigh. Promote a *variant* of the transition table without the clock column, which keeps each rules file coherent but forks a file that must then be kept in step. Or make the clock column conditional in prose — "when time tracking is enabled" — which keeps one file and asks the reader to hold a condition, something this project has found unreliable before (a path-scoped rule that does not load is a rule that does not apply). =org-session-tracking.md= is simpler: it should not promote at all unless the opt-in wiring is chosen.
:END:

The promoted rules tell a consuming session to call `org_clock_in` before its first write and to close the clock on leaving `DOING`. With the wiring cut, those calls queue events nothing consumes. The prose has to say the feature is off by default and what turning it on restores.

*Resolved 2026-09-18: forked at file level, not conditioned in place.* The clock content moved into a new =org-time-tracking.md= that promotes only under =--time=; =org-session-tracking.md= and =org-state-transitions.md= keep what is true regardless of wiring and point at it.


* TODO The SessionStart report mixes stale-interval recovery with the ceremony prompt, and only one half survives :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       6956a46e-da4b-44c2-a701-80196a2930c1
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:15]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:END:
:PLAN:

=session-start-recovery-check= carries two independent reports in one payload, deliberately — "one hook and one payload, because =additionalContext= is a single string and a second SessionStart hook would double the Emacs round-trip".

They fall on opposite sides of this slice. The **stale-interval** half scans for an unclosed =CLOCK:= line and asks what time work stopped: pure time tracking, and with no clocking it can never find anything. The **ceremony** half names pending queue items, drawers out of order and unarchived headings, then asks whether to run the pass: pure task tracking, and a consuming project wants it.

So the hook cannot simply stay or go. Either the stale half is gated off when clocking is, or the two reports separate. The existing =[[ -s ]]= guard already drops an empty payload, so a stale half that always returns nothing degrades silently rather than breaking — which makes "leave it and let it return nothing" a legitimate cheapest option to weigh against the split.
:END:

One `SessionStart` hook carries two independent reports in one `additionalContext` string. Cutting the time-tracking half would take the ceremony prompt with it unless the payload is split first. Establish which half survives the cut and make the surviving one stand alone.

:DEBRIEF:
*Shipped* in [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::e5eb79f][e5eb79f]], one commit with =eb6c4a9b=. Fifteen rows to seven.

*Out* (8): the =queue-append clock_in= and =clock_out= =PostToolUse= matchers; =session-pause= and =clock-target-check= on =Stop=; =session-resume= on =UserPromptSubmit=; =block-start= on =PermissionRequest=; and =block-end= on **both** its events — the unscoped =PostToolUse= matcher and =PermissionDenied=. That last pair is the one worth naming: =block-end= was wired twice, so an inventory counting scripts rather than rows would have undercounted by one. *Staying* (7): =queue-append= for =todo=, =capture= and =amend=; =footnote-check=; =apply-detect=; and both =SessionStart= reports.

*How it was verified, and why not by reading the diff.* The rows were extracted from before and after as =(event, matcher, command)= triples and compared as sets — 8 removed, **0 added**. The "0 added" half is the one a diff read by eye does not give you, and it is what proves no row was silently re-shaped while its neighbours moved. Each removed command was then resolved back to a path and checked present: all six scripts on disk. =jq= parsed the result; =bin/check-conventions= green including "promoted rules match the plugin references"; =bin/test= 688/688, 0 unexpected.

*What could not be verified here, stated rather than glossed.* This repo wires the same scripts through its own =.claude/settings.json=, and never reads =hooks/hooks.json= — the two are mirrors by convention, with nothing mechanical enforcing the mirror (checked: no test and no lint references the file). So the change has **no observable effect in this repo at all**, and no reload makes it observable. Time tracking stays on here, which is intended.

*The claim the plan asked to verify is still unverified, by design.* That a guidepost-free queue yields a review buffer of only state changes, captures and amends is =d3149a81='s member, and this heading deliberately does not pre-empt it.

*One thing the plan did not anticipate.* The manifest's =description= string asserted that it "Mirrors this repo's own .claude/settings.json wiring", which stopped being true the moment the rows came out. It was rewritten to say time tracking ships unwired and name =claude-org-setup --time= as the way back — a forward reference to =1b36c5bd=, which does not exist yet. That is a deliberate small debt, not an oversight: the alternative was a description that described nothing.

*Superseded the same day, 2026-09-18, by* [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::c257553][c257553]]. =hooks/hooks.json= is restored **byte-identically** to its pre-cut state — all fifteen rows, none altered — and the rows now gate themselves on the plugin's =time_tracking= =userConfig= option instead of being absent.

*The premise this member executed was false, and nobody had checked it.* "A hook has no 'disabled' state, so wired and unwired are the only forms available" was the slice's founding sentence and this heading's justification. A third form exists: =userConfig= in =plugin.json=, exported to hook processes as =CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_<KEY>=. Found while researching =1b36c5bd=, whose own problem — where a setup flag could write wiring — evaporated with it.

*What survives intact, which is most of the value.* The row inventory was correct and was reused verbatim to decide which scripts get guards: the eight rows this member removed are exactly the eight that =.claude/settings.json= now prefixes in this repo. The =block-end=-is-wired-twice finding still matters, and still catches an inventory that counts scripts rather than rows. The set-comparison verification method ("8 removed, **0 added**") was what proved the restore was byte-identical, run in the other direction.

*What was wrong was the conclusion only.* Removal was one correct answer to "how do you ship a hook that should not run"; gating is the better one, because it keeps the opt-in to a toggle rather than a file edit and removes the question of whose file gets edited.

*Left DONE rather than reopened.* What this heading did — inventory the rows, verify the change, record why =block-end= appears twice — all happened and is not undone. The reversal is new work, and it belongs to =1b36c5bd=, which owns the opt-in mechanism.
:END:
:PLAN:

The change itself. Out of =hooks/hooks.json=: =session-pause=, =session-resume=, =block-start=, both =block-end= rows, and the =queue-append clock_in= / =queue-append clock_out= matchers. Staying: =queue-append todo=, =capture=, =amend=, =apply-detect=, =footnote-check=, =session-context.sh=.

*Scripts stay on disk, deliberately.* A hook has no "disabled" state — listing it in =hooks.json= is what enables it — so the only two forms available are wired and unwired. Shipping the scripts unwired costs nothing, keeps the opt-in path to one file edit, and means time tracking is *available* rather than removed. It also matches what this repo already does: the same scripts are wired here through =.claude/settings.json=.

*Consequence to verify rather than assume:* with no guideposts in the queue, the review buffer should show only state changes, captures and amends — the item classes that have applied cleanly all along. That is the claim this whole slice rests on and it gets its own member.
:END:

The cut is made in the manifest, not on disk: `hooks/hooks.json` stops wiring the guidepost, permission-block and clock rows, and every script stays where it is so the opt-in can restore them by editing one file. This is the heading that actually performs the disable.

*Resolved 2026-09-18: the manifest drops from 15 hook rows to 7, and no script is deleted.* The plan's inventory proved exact — the eight rows it named are the eight that came out, with =clock-target-check= joining them from =eb6c4a9b= in the same commit.

  removed from shipped wiring in e5eb79f; variant declined as an unasked-for filing mandate
