Split out of [[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]] 2026-08-31,
because it is a concrete one-line change sitting inside a much larger judgement
and does not need to wait for it.

*What is missing.* =bin/hooks/queue-append= writes ts, kind, id, state, from,
note, title, target, tags, text, tool_use_id, session_id, agent_id, agent_type
and source — and no =cwd=. So a queue file says which *session* an event came
from and not which *project*.

*The payload already carries it.* =cwd= was verified present in a captured
=PermissionRequest= payload on 2026-08-14, so this is one more line in the =jq=
object rather than new instrumentation — the same shape as every other field
there.

*Why it is worth recording rather than deriving.* Project is recoverable
indirectly, since =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= encodes it, but
that is a fragile join against a path convention this project does not own,
where a recorded field is free.

*What it unblocks.* Queue files are per-session and all land in
=~/.claude/org-updates/=, which is *user-global* rather than per-project — so
the directory is already the right shape for cross-session comparison, and this
is the field that makes the comparison meaningful. It is one of the two
preconditions [[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]] names; the
other is [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]], without which
only this repo writes queue files at all and there is nothing to compare across.

*Do it before the allocation work, not during it.* The field is only useful on
events written *after* it ships, so every day it is not recorded is a day the
eventual analysis cannot see. That argues for landing it early even though
nothing consumes it yet — the opposite of the usual rule against building unused
machinery, and the reason is that this datum cannot be backfilled.

Sharpened 2026-09-03 by the first concurrent worktree session: a session in
.claude/worktrees/pr8-review writes queue events whose cwd (once recorded) will
differ from the main checkout's while every id-addressed event still resolves
through Emacs against the one live tracker. So cwd is necessary but not
sufficient as project identity — the consumer must normalise a worktree path to
its repository (git worktree list, or the common .git), or events from a
worktree would read as a different project than the tracker they target. Same
session also demonstrated the fragmentation argument against moving the queue
into the repo: each worktree has its own working copy, so a repo-local queue
would scatter events across directories the review pass never reads, while the
shared user-global dir keeps every worktree's events visible to the one apply
pass — which is correct, since they all target the same tracker.

*Merged in from [[id:83b3cdd6-982a-4ae1-878c-79a8c941f638][83b3cdd6]]
2026-09-04, which was the same task written first* (2026-08-15, as
"Record the originating project in queue events"). The two bodies quote
the identical field list and the identical 2026-08-14 payload capture,
and neither cited the other — a textbook twin, caught at review when
both appeared as adjacent slice members. What it adds beyond this body:
the *motivation* (attention allocation needs turn density per project
per hour — an event can say a session was busy but not whose work it
was), the note that the field is a constant today and stops being one
the moment hooks go user-level, and the raw-cwd-versus-slug question —
which this body's 2026-09-03 note already answers: record raw =cwd=,
normalise at consumption (worktree → repository via =git worktree
list= or the common git dir, the same resolution
[[id:f8c86914-0759-4e05-9292-6f7fa5593701][f8c86914]] chose for the
convention checks).

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-04* (commit =2d67b7e=, on
=feature/org-discipline-travel=). One field — =cwd: ($payload.cwd //
null)= — added to =queue-append='s jq object, between =session_id= and
=agent_id=. Test-first per discipline: two assertions added to
=bin/queue-append-test= (a fixture carrying =cwd= must record it; an
absent =cwd= records =null=, not ""), run red against the unmodified
script, then the field turned them green. Full suite passes.

Scope held deliberately tight: the field lands on queue events, which
is what cross-project attribution needs. Two things left for the
consumer, both already argued in the plan above rather than reopened
here — worktree-path normalisation (a worktree's =cwd= differs from its
main checkout while every id-addressed event still resolves against the
one tracker, so the consumer normalises via the common git dir, matching
[[id:f8c86914-0759-4e05-9292-6f7fa5593701][f8c86914]]'s choice), and
whether =claude-code-ide-org--log-event='s audit log should carry the
same field for symmetry. Neither blocks recording the datum now, which
is the point — it cannot be backfilled.


* TODO org_capture's target refuses an 8-character :ID: prefix that every other tool accepts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       25e7b083-360b-4282-8fd0-bb768e2a0f13
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

*Found 2026-08-31 by using it.* Filing four children under
[[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] with
=(claude-code-ide-org-capture TITLE "9e627dc0" ...)= returned =Error: Cannot
find target ID "9e627dc0"= four times. The same prefix resolves fine everywhere
else — =org_body=, =org_amend=, =org_set_todo=, =org_outline='s scope — and the
capture succeeded immediately when handed the full UUID.

*This is the exact defect =--id-find='s own docstring is a monument to*,
recurring at a call site that predates or bypasses it. That docstring records
the first version of the fix expanding prefixes inside =--at-id= only, a commit
message claiming that therefore covered every tool taking an =id=, and it not
being true — =claude-code-ide-org-amend= did its own =org-id-find= first.
*Fourteen such call sites existed.* This is a fifteenth, in the *target*
argument rather than the =id= argument, which is presumably why the sweep missed
it.

*Why it matters more than the keystrokes.* The whole point of prefix expansion
is that a caller never has to produce a full UUID, and this project's memory
rule is to never type one from recall because nine were fabricated across two
sessions with correct prefixes and wrong tails. A tool that demands the full id
pushes the caller straight back into the failure mode the expansion exists to
remove — and the natural workaround is to type it from memory.

*Where to look:* =claude-code-ide-org--capture-target-spec=. It appears to
consult =--id-find= already, so the failure may be a fallback path or an
ordering issue rather than a missing call; the error string it produced is not
=--id-find='s own wording, which is the thread to pull. Worth checking
=org_refile='s =target_id= and =org_divide= for the same shape while there — the
twin question this project asks of any list.

*Cheap verification when fixed:* the four captures above, re-run with the
prefix, plus a test asserting a prefix target resolves. Note the fixture trap
found the same day: =--with-known-ids= leaves its ids unknown to org, so it is
the wrong fixture for anything walking =org-id-locations=.

*A second call site, found 2026-09-01 by using it.*
=claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice='s own =ID= argument compares with =equal=
against the full =:ID:=:

: (equal (downcase (or (org-entry-get nil "ID") "")) (downcase id))

so =(claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice "b36e6369")= matches nothing and returns
=0 slices refreshed, 0 member lines rewritten, 0 blockers updated=. *It looks
exactly like a working no-op* — a plausible answer for a slice that was already
up to date — which is worse than the capture case, where the reply at least said
=Error: Cannot find target ID=.

*So the pattern now has three known shapes*, and the third is the dangerous one:
an argument that resolves through =--id-find= (fine), one that resolves some
other way and *errors* (=org_capture='s target), and one that resolves by string
comparison and *silently selects nothing*. A sweep should look for the third
first.

*Worth checking in the same pass:* every other function taking an =id= or
=target= argument that does not route through =claude-code-ide-org--id-find=.
The docstring on =--id-find= records that fourteen such sites existed the first
time this was fixed; two more have now surfaced by use rather than by search,
which suggests the sweep was by call *shape* rather than by argument.


* TODO A/B the clock record across the phase-3 fixes, since much of what is logged now is bogus :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:10]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 15:11]
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-31, immediately after applying a review pass:* "this
tells that much of our logged activity is bogus data. we should do an A/B
comparison with B starting after this slice or whatever to check for a
qualitative difference."

*The evidence that prompted it, which is unusually good evidence.* The apply
pass that had just been accepted contained a span on
[[id:c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b][c8a97d9d]] rendered as
=[13:53]--[14:04] writes 0:01 in 1= — eleven minutes of headline interval
crediting one minute — for a stretch that was *continuous* work with no idle gap
and no permission block. That heading's work is fully reconstructible from four
commits and a test suite that went 448 to 454, so this is not a case of guessing
whether the number is wrong. It is wrong, and by roughly an order of magnitude,
on a stretch we can independently account for.

The same pass carried =[13:13]--[13:48] writes 0:14 in 3= on
[[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]], which spans an Emacs
crash and an AFK stretch and so is wrong in the *other* direction. Both were
applied before the discrepancy was noticed, so the file now contains them.

*Why this is not simply [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] or
[[id:98c302e0-ab1b-40bf-92d0-d2f2e16ac05d][98c302e0]].* Those two are about the
review buffer *saying* what it means — a headline interval that is not the
interval written, and a readout whose two numbers cannot be told apart. Fixing
them makes the human able to *see* the discrepancy at review time. Neither
claims the underlying attribution is right, and this heading is the question of
whether it is. A perfectly legible rendering of a bogus number is still a bogus
number.

*The design question, and it is the whole task:* what is the B in an A/B where
the thing being measured is accuracy, and there is no ground truth? Some
candidates, none settled:

- *Reconstructible stretches as the yardstick.* Work with commits, test-count
  deltas and a debrief can be bounded from outside the clock system entirely.
  Both spans above were caught precisely because such a record existed. That
  suggests sampling stretches with strong external evidence rather than
  comparing aggregates.
- *Turns per interval*, which
  [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] proposes recording
  anyway, is an independent signal: a span crediting one minute across many
  turns is self-evidently suspect without needing to know the true number.
- *Aggregate shape* — total logged hours per day before and after — is the
  easiest comparison and the weakest, since a change in total does not say which
  direction is right.

*A boundary marker exists, so B has a defined start.* A ends at commit =b34a12f=
(2026-08-31T14:05:07-05:00), the last apply before this was raised. Recording it
here because "after this slice" is not measurable later and a commit is.

*One caution worth stating now, while it is cheap.* If A really is largely
bogus, that is a claim about data already written to =TODO.org= and =DONE.org=,
and the tempting follow-up — correcting or discarding it — should not be reached
for casually. The record's value is partly that it is what actually happened,
mistakes included; [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is this
project's precedent that a plausible fabricated number is worse than an absent
one, and a retroactively "corrected" span is exactly that. Deciding what, if
anything, to do about A is part of this heading and should not be assumed.

*Measured 2026-09-01, in answer to whether this and
[[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] complement each other.
They share a corpus and a histogram; they do not share a design, and the
measurement argues against the before/after framing.*

*B is n=19.* Splitting every queue file at =b34a12f= gives *849* =pause= ->
=resume= gaps before and *19* after — one session. No comparison is available
now and none will be for weeks.

*The bigger problem is a confound, and it was found by testing the obvious
alternative rather than assuming it.* The August band (no gap between 1061s and
2070s) has closed, and the tempting explanation is undersampling: a 150-gap
histogram is sparse, so holes appear by chance. *That is refuted.* Drawing 200
random 150-gap samples from the current corpus, *zero* produced an empty band.
So the August distribution was genuinely different, not merely thinner.

*Which means the gap distribution drifts substantially month to month — and all
of that drift is inside regime A*, since 849 of the 868 gaps predate the
boundary. A before/after comparison on a calendar boundary therefore cannot
attribute a difference to the code: *when* and *what changed* are entangled, and
the =when= term is demonstrably large.

*And there is a second, independent reason to expect no signal.* Almost
everything the slice shipped was *presentational*.
[[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] says so of itself — the
fix "is presentational and deliberately does not touch what is extracted" — and
the same is true of the readout rework, the wording audit, the prefix change and
the marking behaviour. The extraction that produces recorded minutes is
unchanged. The only causal path from the slice to the *numbers* runs through the
human deciding differently at review, which is slow, mediated, and not separable
from drift.

*So the before/after framing should be dropped, and this body already contains
the better instrument.* Its own candidate list opens with /"reconstructible
stretches as the yardstick"/ — work bounded from outside the clock system by
commits, test-count deltas and a debrief. That needs no regime boundary, is
immune to drift, and answers the question actually asked: not "did the numbers
change" but "were they ever right". The two spans that prompted this heading
were caught exactly that way.

*What [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] still contributes*,
and it is real: the same histogram feeds both, and the September occurrence has
to re-derive it anyway. The overlap is the *scan*, not the *comparison*.

*Checked rather than asserted, 2026-09-01, after the claim "nothing changed how
agent time is recorded" was questioned. It was too strong, and the correction
sharpens the conclusion rather than softening it.*

*The computation is byte-identical across the slice.* Every function turning
guideposts into a duration is unchanged against =main=: =--span-work-runs=,
=--apply-idle-floor=, =--aggregate-guideposts=, =--span-turn-seconds=,
=--review-apply-clock=, =--format-clock-line=, =--clock-minutes=,
=--subtract-intervals=.

*Two things do change what lands, though:*

- =--review-apply-item= gained the =inhibit-read-only= binding, so writes that
  previously *failed* now succeed. Not hypothetical — an apply on 2026-08-25
  reported =Applied 0 item(s); 1 failed= for exactly this reason, and the
  ceremony hit it again on 2026-09-01.
- *Auto-marking* ([[id:b6e229c7-3035-4c0e-943b-710a12bfd7d1][b6e229c7]]) means
  items arrive pre-marked, so one =x= applies more per pass — including items a
  human would previously have missed or never marked at all.

\(=--review-items-from-queue= also changed, for the stranded-span drop, but
those items write nothing by definition.)

*Both push B toward more recorded time, which is precisely the wrong direction
for this experiment.* More recorded minutes is not more accurate minutes. A
B-over-A increase in totals would be fully explained by fewer failed writes and
more items applied, with no bearing on whether any individual figure was ever
right — so the one comparison a before/after design makes easy is the one that
cannot be interpreted.

*Same conclusion by a second route:* the question is "were these minutes real",
and only an instrument outside the clock system answers it.


* TODO An Emacs outage strands every org write, because the tools are Emacs :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       3ad389be-2797-46c3-a51d-9f603053955a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 13:50]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 13:52]
:END:

The queue's premise is that a session can record work without a running Emacs:
=bin/hooks/queue-append= is plain shell and jq, and CLAUDE.md says so outright —
"this script must stay runnable with no Emacs at all, which is the premise the
whole queue rests on."

*That premise holds for hooks and fails for tools.* =org_set_todo=,
=org_clock_out= and =org_amend= do not write the queue directly; they are served
by the MCP server running *inside* Emacs, so when Emacs is gone they time out
and record nothing. Meanwhile the guidepost stream keeps flowing, because hooks
are shell — which makes a total failure look partial: =org_pending_updates=
still reports events accumulating while nothing a session actually *decides* can
be written.

*Measured 2026-08-31.* Emacs wedged at ~13:27. The GUI thread took a SIGSEGV
inside =NSAutoFillHeuristicController= calling =-[EmacsMainView selectedRange]=,
which recursed into itself; the Lisp thread then blocked in =mac_select= waiting
on a thread already parked in =deliver_fatal_thread_signal=. Neither could
proceed, so =debug-on-event= was no help either — it needs the Lisp thread to
reach a safe point, and the Lisp thread was the one waiting. Symptom was a
beachball at 99% CPU and =emacsclient= getting *connection refused* from a live
process. Two finished headings could not be closed and two debriefs could not be
written for roughly twenty minutes.

*What it actually cost, stated honestly:* nothing was lost. The debriefs went to
a session scratchpad and were written to org once Emacs came back. But they
survived because a human was present to be told — and a scratchpad file is
exactly the "path to becoming invisible" CLAUDE.md warns about for residue named
only in prose.

*The uncomfortable part, recorded rather than acted on.* Four hours earlier this
same session removed =QUEUE_APPEND_ID/STATE/FROM= from =queue-append=
([[id:46d225c7-7541-4867-af72-929a572df2a5][46d225c7]]) — the one mechanism in
the repo that could write a todo or clock event for a named heading with no
Emacs involved. That removal was correct on its own terms: its consumer had been
dead since the =PLANNING= retirement, and its own tests proved the branch was
live only in the sense of being covered. But the use case that would have wanted
it did not exist when it was written, and does now.

*This heading is a question, not a design.* Restoring the overrides is the
obvious move and probably the wrong one: a synthetic event has no
=tool_response= to recover =from= from, so the staleness check =recovered_from=
feeds would go blind on exactly the events written when the system is least
healthy. Worth deciding among at least:

1. Tools fail *loudly and durably* rather than silently — a timed-out MCP write
   leaves a marker that the next =SessionStart= reports, so the record of what
   could not be recorded is itself in the system. This is the option that
   composes with the existing recovery report rather than adding a second path.
2. A narrow, non-hook CLI writer for todo/clock on a known =:ID:=, with =from=
   honestly null rather than fabricated.
3. Nothing, on the grounds that an Emacs crash is rare and a human was present.
   This is a real option and may well be the right one — today's cost was two
   debriefs delayed, not lost.

Whatever is chosen, the shape to avoid is the current one, where the failure is
silent per call and only a human noticing a timeout keeps the work attached to
its heading.

*Related but distinct:* [[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]] is
about =org_capture= and =org_set_property= hanging over MCP *while Emacs is
healthy and other tools answer*. This one is about every tool failing because
Emacs is not there at all. They were confirmed separate on 2026-08-31: after the
restart, =org_capture= over MCP timed out again and persisted nothing — buffer
unmodified, not read-only, no prompt open, =recursion-depth= 0 — while the same
function through =emacsclient= completed in under a second.


* TODO An archive pass wrote one subtree twice, and the mechanism is still unknown :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       381ea07d-633f-4640-abbb-89472a07acba
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:END:

On 2026-08-31 a live archive pass reported 65 and wrote 66. One story and its
six children landed twice in DONE.org, ids and all. It is guarded but not
understood.

*The arithmetic says where it is not.* =n= increments once per
=org-archive-subtree= call and the pass reported 65, so exactly 65 calls ran and
one pasted its subtree twice -- inside the call, on org's own paste path.
Double-consumption of a marker would have reported 66. The heading it duplicated
was also the last in the file and therefore the *first* consumed under
reverse-order iteration, so no earlier deletion existed to disturb its marker.
The marker-collapse explanation is excluded.

*It did not reproduce.* Same file, same code, fresh buffer: 65 archived, no
duplicates. So it is a property of the live buffer's state rather than of the
data. That buffer had been reverted, had its read-only flag cleared, and had
just been rewritten by drawer consolidation and separation normalisation in the
same ceremony.

*What stands in for a fix.* =claude-code-ide-org-archive-finished= now checks
the result rather than the method: after a sweep, no =:ID:= may appear twice in
the archive target, or it signals -- which the ceremony turns into a held
repeater and a reported failure. Every other signal looked right at the time,
the count and the levels included; only counting ids found it.

Worth attempting first: re-run the sweep against a buffer put through the same
sequence -- revert, consolidate, normalise, then archive -- to see whether the
combination is what does it.


* TODO [0/2] Make an archived day hold that day's record :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f9fe9fac-a423-4ede-bc42-1d201c2c3d82
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:07]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:KIND:     slice
:COOKIE_DATA: checkbox recursive
:BLOCKER:  d6fc47a5-b84d-4287-b973-aa5b37410270 591e7af3-4f37-4f90-86ba-62400c6e649b
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:07]
:END:

Seeded 2026-08-31 from the proposal reviewed in conversation: DONE.org's
datetree should gather what happened on a day, not only what was filed. Both
members move *records* into it rather than changing what a task is.

*Its work is blocked from outside this slice.*
[[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]] builds the datetree and
belongs to [[id:b36e6369-a210-4540-adc7-0c0c188e9791][b36e6369]], so nothing
here starts until that lands. That is precisely why these two were kept *out* of
b36e6369 rather than added to it: including them would have left a 27-member
slice unable to close until its own downstream consumers were done too.

*Use org's own datetree machinery, and note which entry point.*
=org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= takes a comparison function per level and
=org-datetree-comparefun-from-regex= builds one; negating its result gives the
descending order [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]] asks for.
Measured in batch 2026-08-31 -- entries inserted 08-10, 08-31, 07-05, 08-20
landed in strict descending order at year, month and day. Only
=org-datetree-find-date-create= hard-codes ascending, which is what made a
hand-rolled builder look necessary at first.

*What a day node means, and does not.* It holds what org would allocate to that
date. A task's *earlier* clock intervals stay where they were recorded, so a day
node is not a substitute for a clocktable when the question is how much time
went where.

Planned:

- [ ] [[id:d6fc47a5-b84d-4287-b973-aa5b37410270][d6fc47a5]] TODO Distribute Review and planning's past day nodes into DONE.org's datetree
- [ ] [[id:591e7af3-4f37-4f90-86ba-62400c6e649b][591e7af3]] TODO Bound Review attention's LOGBOOK by refiling old entries into dated clones


* TODO Bound Review attention's LOGBOOK by refiling old entries into dated clones :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       591e7af3-4f37-4f90-86ba-62400c6e649b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 11:21]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 11:22]
:END:

Keep =* Review attention=
([[id:335dfa97-c1b0-4e98-804f-81495eb63f20][335dfa97]]) exactly as it is, and
stop its =:LOGBOOK:= growing without bound by *refiling old entries into
clones*. For each day at or before yesterday, clone the heading into that day's
node in DONE.org's datetree and move that day's CLOCK lines into the clone. The
clones are archival records, not tasks, and carry no =:ID:=.

*What this deliberately does not do, since an earlier reading of the proposal
got it backwards.* It does not restructure the live heading into day nodes. So
the clock target is unchanged: =claude-code-ide-org-review-attention-heading=
still resolves to this heading by title, =--review-attention-start= still opens
the clock on it, and neither needs touching. =--ceremony-reviewed-today-p= is
likewise unaffected, because it looks for a CLOCK line dated *today* and only
previous-day-or-older lines ever move.

*The cost, which is the trade and should be written down rather than
discovered.* A clocktable scoped to this heading stops seeing the moved
intervals -- they live in the clones from then on. Any report of review
attention *over time* has to sum the clones too. That is the price of a bounded
=:LOGBOOK:=, and it is worth paying, but a report built later against this
heading alone will quietly under-count.

*Two things to check before writing it.* Clones with no =:ID:= and no
=:CREATED:= are a deliberate exception to the every-heading rule, so confirm
=bin/lint-org= does not demand either at that depth. And splitting a =:LOGBOOK:=
by day is the highest-risk edit shape in this repo's history -- two body
corruptions on record from hand-rolled region edits -- so it wants a tested
function rather than a step inside a sweep.

Idempotence is the same requirement as the sibling heading: a re-run must merge
into the day's existing clone, never append a second.


* TODO Distribute Review and planning's past day nodes into DONE.org's datetree :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d6fc47a5-b84d-4287-b973-aa5b37410270
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 11:21]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:BLOCKER:  33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 11:22]
:END:

Move every =Review and planning= datetree node dated the previous day or older
into the matching datetree leaf in DONE.org, as part of the archiving procedure.
Today's node stays; it is still accumulating.

*Use org's own machinery -- it does support a descending tree.*
=org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= takes a comparison function per level, and
=org-datetree-comparefun-from-regex= builds one from a regex; negating its
result gives descending order. Measured in batch on 2026-08-31: entries inserted
08-10, 08-31, 07-05, 08-20 landed in strict descending order at year, month and
day. Use that rather than =org-datetree-find-date-create=, which hard-codes
ascending comparators and is the reason a hand-rolled builder looked necessary
at first.

*What a day node means, read against org's own allocation conventions* -- it
holds what org would file to that date: entries whose =CLOSED:= falls that day,
and clock activity recorded that day. The only caveat worth writing down is
small: a task's *earlier* clock intervals stay where they were recorded, so a
day node is not a substitute for a clocktable when the question is how much time
went where.

*Merge, do not append.* Running twice must find the existing day and merge into
it -- the same problem drawer consolidation already solved.

*Retire the broken =:ARCHIVE:= property on the way past.* =* Review and
planning= carries =:ARCHIVE: DONE.org::* Review and planning= and no such
heading exists in DONE.org, measured 2026-08-31. Archiving it today would
silently append a second near-identical heading at end of file, the documented
failure of a literal-string target. This work replaces that route, so it should
remove the property rather than leave a live trap behind.

Once the sibling heading refiles =* Review attention='s old LOGBOOK entries into
dated clones, those clones are placed by this same pass.


* MAYBE Install orgparse for ad-hoc analysis, and stop reaching for regex over org text :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2c5f7c50-40c9-4d06-b435-add41eb97643
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 15:58]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "MAYBE"      from              [2026-08-28 Fri 15:58] \\
  offered; not yet installed or decided
:END:

*Offered by the user 2026-08-28:*
[[https://github.com/karlicoss/orgparse][orgparse]] is available and could be
installed system-wide for Claude to use from Python. Not installed as of that
date; =python3= here is 3.14.6.

*The evidence for it is two bugs from a single session, both the same shape.*
Extracting a heading's =:ID:= by regex returned =\"...\"== — the pattern matched
a line in the *previous* heading's body. And a scan for level-1 headings lacking
=:CREATED:= walked *into* the datetree, so the anchor inherited a day node's
timestamp; it reported *0* when the answer was *1*, and was caught only because
the number contradicted something seen ten minutes earlier. Both are
entry-boundary errors, which is precisely the class a real parser removes.

*The sharper finding is about practice rather than the library, and it is the
reason this is =MAYBE= rather than =TODO=.* Emacs was the better instrument in
both cases and was under-used: =org-map-entries= bounds entries by construction,
and it is the *authoritative* parser — the one that actually writes these files.
A Python parser is a second implementation of org's semantics that can disagree
with the implementation of record, which is the "second copy that can disagree"
this slice exists to close. The org-dev skill's §0b rule already says to check
what org has before reaching elsewhere, and =python3= was reached for roughly
forty times in that session without the question being asked once.

*So the split that would make it worth installing:*

- *Emacs for anything semantic* — entry boundaries, property inheritance,
  keyword sets, clock arithmetic, agenda behaviour. It is running, and org's own
  answer is the answer.
- *orgparse for where Emacs is not the right instrument*: when it is down (it
  was, that morning), whole-corpus counting without touching a live buffer, and
  transformations worth diffing on a scratch copy before they go near the real
  file — the [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] taxonomy pass
  was exactly that.

*One boundary, cheap to state now and expensive to retrofit: nothing checked
into =bin/= imports it without declaring it.* This repo has *zero* Python today
— every script is bash, fish or elisp — so it would be a new dependency class,
and [[id:1ed7b2b4-a809-4547-b04f-099a3111fc1e][1ed7b2b4]] is the standing scar
about an undeclared one. Ad-hoc session analysis is free; a committed script
would need a declaration and a check in =bin/check-org-dev-skill= beside the
org-version one.

*If it is installed*, the org-dev skill should record both halves — that
orgparse exists, and that Emacs remains the answer wherever org's own semantics
decide the result. Recording only the first half would turn a tool that fixes a
bug class into a tool that introduces a worse one.

*A third instance of the bug class, 2026-09-03, and it argues for the practice
rather than the library — so this stays =MAYBE=.* Looking up eight headings'
exact titles for a footnote list, a Python regex keyed on =:ID:= attributed
three of them to the wrong heading, because the pattern matched an id quoted
inside an *earlier* heading's body. Same entry-boundary error as the two above.
The fix was not a parser: it was anchoring on the heading's own property drawer
— which is what =org-map-entries= does by construction, in the process that
actually writes these files.

So the split this heading proposes holds, and the evidence keeps landing on the
Emacs side of it. Considered for promotion while composing =ff7ccb2d= and
deliberately not promoted: promoting it would adopt the option its own body
argues against.

Reconsidered at =ff7ccb2d='s Step 6 (2026-09-08): the user chose to
keep it =MAYBE= over the recommended cancel — parked, not dead, in case
ad-hoc analysis outgrows what the tools and =org-map-entries= cover.


* TODO Two keyword-set proposals and the review collision are still unadopted :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       dbf70459-bde4-4550-bd81-0d4f9ebc50e3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 13:39]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 13:40] \\
  residue carried out of c954f650
:END:

*Filed 2026-08-28 as [[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]
closed.* That heading asked whether the whole keyword progression earns its
keep, answered the =PLANNING= half by retiring it, and left three questions
open. They are carried out here rather than left in a closed heading's =:PLAN:=
drawer, which readers are told to skip — the exact path to invisibility
CLAUDE.md's filing rule exists to block.

*1. Get =MAYBE= out of the default agenda without moving it after the =|=.* At
the 2026-08-10 census it was 23 of 56 keyworded headings, the largest state — a
healthy Someday/Maybe list rendered *actionable* by sitting before the
separator. GTD is emphatic that Someday/Maybe lives outside the active system
and is reviewed periodically. Moving it past the =|= would be semantically
wrong, since these are not finished; Bernt Hansen's answer is to keep such
states active and filter them from default agenda views. Worth re-counting
before deciding — the corpus has roughly tripled since.

*2. Narrow =WAITING= to "waiting on a person".* GTD separates *Waiting For* —
delegated, needs chasing — from *blocked*, a dependency. Dependencies here are
already structural, via =:BLOCKER:=, so the current gloss overlaps it. Recorded
when the keyword had never been used once; that is no longer true, so the
evidence has changed and should be re-read rather than reused.

*3. The word "review" means three things.* The =:review:= tag (a kind of work),
=claude-code-ide-org-review= (the command that applies the queue), and the
=REVIEW= state. "The review buffer shows a REVIEW item tagged =:review:=" is a
sentence someone will have to write. Either pick a different word for one of
them or adopt the collision deliberately and say so in CLAUDE.md — the one thing
not to do is leave it unremarked, which is where it stands.

*Note what is no longer open, so this is not re-litigated.* Whether =DOING=
should exist at all was *settled* in c954f650 and the reasoning is in its
=:PLAN:= drawer: the org+GTD tradition argues against an in-progress keyword
because the clock can say what is active, and that substitute is exactly what
the event queue removes here. A future reader citing Bernt or GTD has a
well-supported argument and is wrong for this system specifically. =REVIEW='s
adoption and =PLANNING='s retirement are likewise decided.


* TODO Orient the datetree newest-at-top, by re-sorting after day-node creation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Meta
:ID:       0c3f1319-67c0-4b29-a8e7-5ffa12948aaf
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 14:57]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 14:58] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
CLOCK: [2026-08-26 Wed 15:33]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:36] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-26 Wed 15:33]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:36] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-26 Wed 15:43]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:44] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-26 Wed 15:43]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:44] assigned at review
:END:

*Requested by the user 2026-08-26*, while noticing that the
[[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] flattening analysis "both
forgot about datetrees". The ask was for a custom sort function giving the
datetree a newest-at-top orientation, to match the same recency ordering
proposed for TODO.org and DONE.org.

*No custom sort function is needed, measured.* =(org-sort-entries nil ?A)= on
the month node produces =("2026-08-21" "2026-08-17" "2026-08-14")= in one call.
Org's day nodes are ISO-prefixed, so lexical order *is* date order and
reverse-alphanumeric *is* newest-first. Same for month nodes within a year and
year nodes within the anchor. The comparator this needs already ships.

*The work is placement, not comparison.* =org-datetree-find-date-create= assumes
ascending and inserts accordingly, so a newly created node lands in the wrong
place in a descending tree -- measured: filing 2026-08-25 into a descending
month appended it at the *bottom*. One =org-sort-entries= call afterwards
corrects it, verified end to end:

: before create : ("08-21" "08-17" "08-14")
: after create  : ("08-21" "08-17" "08-14" "08-25")
: after resort  : ("08-25" "08-21" "08-17" "08-14")

*So the change is a re-sort at one call site*,
=claude-code-ide-org--ensure-day-node=, which already creates the node, stamps
=:CREATED:=, takes an =:ID:= and saves. Sorting the three scaffolding levels
before that save is contained and idempotent.

*The hazard, which is real but not on this project's path.* Raw
=org-datetree-find-date-create= does a *sorted scan with early exit*, so against
a descending tree it passes the target, concludes the date is absent, and
creates a **duplicate day node**. Measured: filing an already-present 2026-08-17
produced =("08-17" "08-21" "08-17" "08-14")=. Two nodes for one day, each taking
its own =:ID:=, would split that day's meta-work clock silently -- the worst
available failure for this subsystem.

*It does not reach us because =--ensure-day-node= never calls org's finder
blind.* It looks the node up first via =claude-code-ide-org--find-day-node=,
which scans *every* heading in the subtree matching on exact rendered title,
with no early exit and no order assumption. Verified against a descending tree:
all three present days found, a nonexistent one correctly nil. Org's finder is
reached only when the day genuinely does not exist, which is the one case where
insertion order cannot duplicate anything.

*What remains exposed is anything that bypasses that wrapper* -- an
=org-capture= template targeting =file+olp+datetree=, or a hand =C-c C-j=.
Neither is configured here today. If the tree is reoriented, that becomes a
standing constraint worth writing down rather than a latent bug: *the day node
is created through =--ensure-day-node= or not at all.*

*Independent of the flattening decision.* This is wanted whether or not
[[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] goes ahead, and touches
none of the same code.

*Superseded [2026-08-26 Wed], before anything was built.* The
re-sort-after-creation plan above is a workaround for a limitation org removed
in 9.8. Use the supported extension point instead.

*=org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= takes a comparison function per level*,
and =org-datetree-comparefun-from-regex= is public specifically to build them.
The find-or-create scan is driven *entirely* by that comparator --
=--find-create-subheading= walks siblings for the first one where the result is
=>= 0=, inserts before it, and detects an existing entry by the same function
returning =0= or =t=. So inverting the comparator inverts insertion *and* lookup
together, which is why the duplicate hazard disappears rather than being worked
around.

*Measured on org 9.8.7, comparator simply negated:*

| filed date       | found existing | resulting order         |
|------------------+----------------+-------------------------|
| 08-25 (newest)   | nil, created   | 25, 21, 17, 14 -- top   |
| 08-16 (interior) | nil, created   | 21, 17, 16, 14 -- slot  |
| 08-17 (present)  | *t*            | unchanged, no duplicate |

*So the change is a call-site swap, not a sort pass.* =--ensure-day-node=
currently calls the legacy =org-datetree-find-date-create=, which hardcodes
ascending. Passing three descending comparators to
=org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= with ='subtree-at-point= replaces it. The
=--find-day-node= title pre-check can stay -- it is order-independent and cheap
-- but it stops being load-bearing, since org's own finder now handles the
existing-date case correctly.

*The community history, since it explains why this took sixteen years.* Evan
asked for exactly this on emacs-orgmode in April 2010 -- "a date tree with the
recent dates on the top in contrast to the normal date tree". Carsten Dominik
declined it as deliberate rather than missing: "I have a hard time why, if you
are creating a new node every day, you'd like the subnodes first on top." The
gap was filled out-of-tree by =org-reverse-datetree= (akirak), still on MELPA.

*And it was never granted on its own merits.* What landed in 9.8 is a
generalisation of =:tree-type= to arbitrary subsets of =(year quarter month week
day)= plus arbitrary functions, motivated by lunar, academic and retail 4-4-5
calendars. Reverse ordering is an *incidental* capability of the comparator seam
that generalisation required. Worth knowing, because it means there is no
first-class =org-datetree-reversed= option to wait for and probably never will
be -- the seam is the answer org intends.

*Consequence for the flattening question*
([[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]]): none. This is
orthogonal and was already noted there as independent.


* NEXT A clocktable never shows a parent's own time, only own-plus-subtree :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       64d34a64-d336-40a8-a8e8-da8133d0da06
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 14:46]
:BLOCKER:  406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 14:46] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-26 Wed 14:47] \\
  direct continuation of what 3964c575 decided
:END:

*The reporting half of [[id:3964c575-50bc-4cc9-abeb-bdd1c0162c2c][3964c575]],
split out when that heading was decided 2026-08-26.* That one asked whether a
grouping may carry its own clock; the answer is yes, a parent's own coordination
time is real work. This is what makes that answer *legible*.

*Measured, not inferred.* A parent carrying 1:00 of its own, with children of
2:00 and 0:30:

: | A story        | 3:30 |      |
: | \_  Child one  |      | 2:00 |
: | \_  Child two  |      | 0:30 |

*The parent's own 1:00 appears nowhere.* This is worse than the usual framing,
which is that own time is "mixed in and cannot be told apart" -- it is not
printed at all. It is recoverable only as =parent_row - sum(child_rows)=, which
is arithmetic the reader performs silently and which =:maxlevel= truncation
breaks without warning: cap the depth and the subtraction quietly returns the
wrong number, with nothing on the page indicating that it did.

*So the 1:08 on [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] was never
a rounding artefact.* 3964c575's body recorded "parent row 14:52, ten children
summing 13:44, the 1:08 difference indistinguishable from a rounding artefact".
It was the parent's own two CLOCK lines, and it was indistinguishable only
because the table declines to name it.

*No native parameter does this.* Checked in org 9.8.7: the clocktable has no
own-time or non-recursive option, and =:properties= only adds property columns,
not a derived quantity. =org-clock-sum= is recursive by definition and writes
one =CLOCKSUM= per heading.

*Scope: presentation only, and deliberately so.* Nothing about what is
extracted, stored or applied changes. Candidate shapes, none chosen -- a
=:formatter= adding an own-time column; a project-level clocktable wrapper; or
surfacing it only where it matters rather than in every table. Whether every
parent row wants a second number, or only the nine that currently carry own
lines, is the first thing to settle.

*Nine headings currently make this visible*, measured 2026-08-26: 46 own CLOCK
lines across four active and five finished groupings.

*Re-measured 2026-09-15 at the user's question — still true, and the
population is not frozen.* **Ten** groupings now carry their own CLOCK
lines, **54** of them, against the nine and 46 recorded above. The growth
is the mechanism working as the state-transitions rules describe rather
than new clocking of groupings: a leaf clocked honestly acquires children
and becomes a parent.

*But the live exposure is two, not ten.* Eight of the ten are =DONE= and
archived, where a clocktable is rarely run; only
[[id:406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4][406e9200]] (=DOING=, 4 own lines) and
[[id:a1d63f52-4c07-4b9e-8f31-6d2b90c4e7a5][a1d63f52]] (=TODO=, 9) are live. So
the defect is real and its blast radius is small — which is the honest reading,
and narrower than "ten groupings" sounds.

*One of those two makes this timely.* =406e9200= is the story
[[id:749301a0-0bd0-4fbd-b3f7-79739226e15a][749301a0]] sequences, and that slice
is second of the four on =next-session.md=. Whoever works it will be reading
clock data for precisely one of the ambiguous parents.

*What changed against this heading, and it lowers priority without
touching correctness.* When this was written the clocktable was the
project's reporting surface. Since =afea7e4f= there are two others —
=claude-code-ide-org-attention-report= and
=claude-code-ide-org-allocation-report= — and **neither uses a clocktable
or inherits this defect**. The questions a clocktable was being asked are
partly answered elsewhere now, so the case for a =:formatter= is weaker
than it was even though the gap it would close is unchanged.

*Unchanged and re-confirmed:* org 9.8.7 still offers no own-time or
non-recursive clocktable option, and nothing shipped in =afea7e4f= goes
near =org-clock-sum=.

*Blocked on [[id:406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4][406e9200]], set
2026-09-15.* The open question above — "whether every parent row wants a second
number, or only the [ten] that currently carry own lines" — is a design decision
with no evidence behind it yet, and the evidence is about to be generated:
=406e9200= is one of the two live groupings with own time, and working its
remaining children means reading exactly the ambiguous number. Either that work
needs the own-time figure, which justifies the =:formatter=, or it works around
it, which shows the gap is tolerable. Both are better inputs than deciding now.

*The blocker names the story rather than the slice that sequences it.*
[[id:749301a0-0bd0-4fbd-b3f7-79739226e15a][749301a0]] is what will drive the
work, but a slice is a *declared* grouping that can be re-composed, dropped or
split, where the story is the heading whose completion actually constitutes the
evidence. Blocking on the more stable of the two is the point of preferring a
property over a prose sentence.

*Note the blocker is inert until
[[id:3f4fd744-4ba7-4be8-9fe4-54e46119515a][3f4fd744]] lands.* =org_set_property=
writes the =ids(...)= wrapper, which =org-depend-block-todo= cannot parse, so
this refuses nothing today — which is the defect that heading exists to fix,
demonstrating itself on the very next property written after it was measured.


* TODO A guidepost gap containing an apply timestamp is provably the human's attention :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       996fd2bd-be90-44b3-b2c5-9018255940b3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 10:09]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 10:10] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Found 2026-08-26* while the user asked whether a span they had flipped to
active notation "fits the data", and whether such spans should always be offered
that way.

*The question a =pause=--=resume= gap cannot answer on its own.* Such a gap says
the *agent* was not running. Human attention is a subset of that and the record
has never been able to separate them -- the sleeping-laptop gap of 2026-08-25
16:56--17:50 is byte-identical in shape to an hour of careful reviewing.

*But an apply timestamp inside the gap settles it.*
[[id:21c91613-76e0-4590-affe-e2af9f1321ca][21c91613]] made every consumed event
carry the time of the pass that consumed it, so "was a review pass running
between 09:49 and 09:59?" is now a lookup. An apply cannot happen without a
human at =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review=; it is the one act in this system that
is *definitionally* human. So a gap containing one provably contained human
attention.

*Three cases, and it discriminates on all three:*

: 09:23:09--09:29:06   contains pass 09:23:26   -> human
: 09:49:22--09:59:16   contains pass 09:56:09   -> human
: 16:56   --17:50      (2026-08-25, laptop asleep)   no pass   -> not human

The third is the important one: it is the known negative, confirmed
independently by =Display is turned off/on= and by scratch-file mtimes, and the
rule correctly declines it.

*So the answer to "should they always be offered as active" is no, and the
qualifier is mechanisable.* Not every gap -- only one containing an apply. That
is a predicate over data the queue already holds, not a judgement, which is what
makes it offerable rather than merely arguable.

*What it would change.* [[id:01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0][01849bef]]
asks for a keystroke because flipping the notation by hand is tedious. This says
the review pass can often skip the asking: when a span contains an apply
timestamp, render it active and say why. The keystroke remains for the cases the
predicate cannot reach -- reading, thinking, deciding without applying anything
-- which are real and are exactly what
[[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]]'s clock now captures going
forward.

*Its limits, stated so it is not over-trusted.* It finds only gaps in which
someone *applied*; a pass that reviews carefully and dismisses everything writes
no apply timestamp and would be missed, which is the same blind spot the "apply
completes" stop-signal had in
[[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]]'s design. And it is
retrospective by nature: it explains gaps already recorded rather than measuring
attention as it happens.

*Worth applying to the backlog once*, since the ledger reaches back to
2026-08-18 and the gaps are all still there to be scored.

*First measurement, 2026-09-16, prompted by the user asking whether 0-run spans
are "always mine to claim" and, if so, why they are not offered that way.*

**The predicate reaches none of them, and the reason is a population mismatch
rather than a flaw.** Scored against the currently offered items: **13 zero-run
spans totalling 27 minutes, and 0 of them contain an apply timestamp.**

*Why zero, stated so the result is not read as a failure of the rule.* This
heading's three worked cases are gaps of 6 and 10 minutes around review passes.
The 0-run spans actually on offer today have envelopes of **8, 5, 3, 3, 2, 1, 1,
1, 1, 1, 1, 1 and 1 minutes** — median **one minute**. They are the pauses
*between an agent's turns*, not the gaps around a human's review pass. Nobody
runs =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= inside a one-minute gap, so the predicate
correctly declines every one. It was built for a different population and still
answers that one; it simply does not answer this one.

*The ledger has a horizon, which this heading's "apply to the backlog once"
should account for.* Watermark files come in two shapes: **17 apply-pass stamps
are known, and 14 files predate =21c91613= entirely**, holding a bare vector of
event timestamps with no pass time at all. A backlog scoring pass can therefore
score only the newer half, and the older half is not merely unscored but
unscoreable. Some newer entries also carry an *empty* pass time.

*What this does not change:* the negative that makes this heading necessary
still stands, and is the answer to the user's question. A =pause=--=resume= gap
says the *agent* was idle; the 2026-08-25 16:56--17:50 sleeping-laptop gap is
byte-identical in shape to an hour of careful reviewing. So 0-run spans are
**not** always the human's to claim, and defaulting them to claimed would trade
a silent undercount for a silent overcount — with no gain in visibility, since a
wrong CLOCK line looks exactly like a right one.

*What it does suggest:* for the *short* population above, absence is implausible
in a way it is not at 54 minutes, so a width-bounded default is the shape worth
arguing about. Filed separately rather than decided here, since where that bound
falls is a reporting decision.


* TODO Flipping a span's timestamps to active is hand-editing; give the review buffer a key :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0

CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 12:11]--[2026-09-16 Wed 12:12] =>  0:01

CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 12:12]--[2026-09-16 Wed 12:13] =>  0:01
- <2026-09-16 Wed 12:12>--<2026-09-16 Wed 12:13> assigned at review

*Requested by the user 2026-08-26:* "consider adding a keystroke to org-review
that flips an inactive interval to active. that edit is tedious for me."

*What is tedious, concretely.* A span is rendered with *inactive* timestamps,
and turning =[2026-08-26 Wed 08:12]--[2026-08-26 Wed 08:31]= into the active
form means editing four bracket characters by hand in two places, after the item
has already been assigned. The review buffer has =e= for editing an interval's
*times* but nothing for its *kind*.

*Why it is inactive today, and why the request is not simply a display
preference.* =b8e6007a= (in DONE.org) settled that the queue records *agent*
activity and the agenda must not absorb it, and the active branch was dropped
from =--review-format-annotation= outright. So flipping to active is not a
formatting choice -- it asserts *this interval was the human's attention*, which
is a different quantity and the one thing the agenda should show.

*Which makes this the manual half of
[[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]].* That heading proposes
clocking the human's review attention from the review command itself. Until it
exists, the human is doing by hand exactly what it would automate, and the same
day this was requested the user observed they had "forgotten to represent my
attention as active spans" -- so the tedium is already producing omissions, not
just friction.

*Design questions worth settling before binding a key.*
- *Does the flip mean "this was mine" or merely "show this in the agenda"?* If
  the former, the marker should probably be recorded on the item rather than
  inferred from bracket style, and the rendering follow from it.
- *What does it apply to?* A single item, the marked set, or every span in a
  cluster. Marking a whole review pass as the human's attention in one keystroke
  is likely the real want.
- *Which key.* =a= is assign, =e= is edit-interval, =N= is note, =d= is dismiss.
  =t= (for *type*) and =A= (active) both look free; check the keymap before
  choosing rather than trusting this list.

*Do not let this quietly become
[[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]]'s substitute.* A key that
makes the manual path bearable reduces the pressure to automate it, and the
automated version is strictly better -- it knows when the review command was
running, which a human retrofitting brackets afterwards is reconstructing from
memory.

*Correction [2026-08-26 Wed]: the premise above is stale.* "Until it exists, the
human is doing by hand exactly what it would automate" was written while
[[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]] was open. It is now =DONE=
-- the review pass clocks itself onto =Review attention=
([[id:335dfa97-c1b0-4e98-804f-81495eb63f20][335dfa97]]), and the command *is*
the event. The automated path exists, so this heading is no longer the only way
the human's attention gets represented.

*Which sharpens this heading's own closing caution rather than retiring it.* "Do
not let this quietly become 961f15b6's substitute" was a warning about a future;
it is now a live constraint. A key that flips brackets by hand is *worse* than
what already ships for any interval a review pass covers, because the pass knows
when it was running while a human retrofitting brackets afterwards is
reconstructing from memory. Binding the key for that case would be building the
inferior half of a thing already built.

*So the question narrows to a real one: what attention is neither an agent turn
nor a review pass?* Measured 2026-08-26 rather than supposed. The span
=[2026-08-26 Wed 13:05]--[2026-08-26 Wed 13:22]= is backed by six guideposts and
nothing else. =--span-work-runs= extracts *one minute* of agent work from it,
correctly, since only a =resume= -> =pause= adjacency counts. The other fourteen
minutes are the user reading a session report: real attention, on this project,
captured by nothing. Not agent activity, so
[[id:b8e6007a-ae13-4b13-bb2e-a5df39435af0][b8e6007a]] rightly keeps
queue-derived spans out of the agenda; not a review pass, so 961f15b6's clock
never opened.

*That gap is the actual want behind the original request*, and it is worth
separating from the tedium that prompted it. The user's words were "that edit is
tedious for me" and, the same day, that they had "forgotten to represent my
attention as active spans". The tedium argues for a key. The forgetting argues a
key will not fix it either -- an omission caused by having to remember is not
repaired by making the remembering cheaper.

*Two neighbours now carry pieces of this.*
[[id:996fd2bd-be90-44b3-b2c5-9018255940b3][996fd2bd]] proposes reclaiming such a
gap when an apply timestamp falls inside it, which is the automatic answer to
the same question. [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] records
that the review buffer *names* an item by that 17-minute window while writing
only the 1-minute payload -- so today the readout implies the gap is already
accounted for, which is part of why it goes unnoticed.

*Still open and unchanged by any of this*: the three design questions above --
what the flip *means*, what it applies to, and which key.

*The user added the purpose and the limit in one sentence, 2026-09-04:* "a
keystroke to toggle active/inactive would be useful … and presenting certain
spans as active when it's warranted would be an aid to interpretation. **I'm
sure I will miss the signs too often.**"

*The first clause is this heading and needs no change.* The second names a
second mechanism, and the third is the argument that the second is not optional.

**A keystroke only converts a judgement already made.** It removes the four
bracket characters, not the noticing — and the noticing is the part that fails.
The same person, in the same session, said of their =AFK= discipline that they
"pop in, make a quick judgment or queue a prompt during a long-running turn, and
then pop out again without mentioning 'still AFK'" (=409a69ff=). A reviewer who
does not reliably emit the marker will not reliably spot its consequence twenty
spans later either. So the manual half is *necessary and insufficient*, and
shipping only it would look like progress while leaving the failure mode
untouched.

*What the second mechanism is, and why it is not this heading.* Presenting a
span as already active where the evidence warrants it is a classification, and
whether a class exists that is unambiguous enough to mark without asking is an
empirical question with a scheduled answer: =96a51c2f=, monthly from 2026-09-13,
now carries it. Deciding here would be guessing ahead of the data.

*The interaction worth designing for either way:* if some spans arrive
pre-marked, the keystroke stops being "assert attention" and becomes "correct
the machine" — a different act, on a smaller number of items, with the default
carrying most of the weight. Worth knowing before the key is bound, because it
changes what the reviewer is being asked to do.

*A defect this heading would ship on day one, found 2026-09-04 when the user
flipped an interval by hand and the review offered it again as inactive.*

**An active CLOCK line is invisible to the idempotency check.**
=--recorded-intervals-at-point= is what keeps a replay from writing an interval
twice, and it matches =claude-code-ide-org--clock-line-regexp=, which is
hard-anchored on square brackets:

: "\\`[ \t]*CLOCK: \\[\\([^]]+\\)\\]--\\[\\([^]]+\\)\\][ \t]*=>[ \t]*[0-9]+:[0-9][0-9][ \t]*\\'"

Measured in batch, same drawer, same interval, one character different at each
end:

| line                                                       | recognised |
|------------------------------------------------------------+------------|
| =CLOCK: [2026-09-04 Fri 10:16]--[2026-09-04 Fri 10:21]=     | one interval |
| =CLOCK: <2026-09-04 Fri 10:16>--<2026-09-04 Fri 10:21>=     | **nil**      |

*So the flip this heading exists to automate un-records the interval it is
applied to.* Nothing is subtracted, the span is offered again, and applying it
writes a **second** line for the same minutes — which every clocktable then
sums, the exact overcount =claude-code-ide-org--subtract-intervals= was written
to prevent. A keystroke would reproduce this reliably rather than occasionally,
since its whole purpose is to make the flip cheap.

*Which sharpens the scope: the key is not the work.* Whatever recognises a
recorded interval has to recognise both kinds — and then decide whether an
*active* one still subtracts, which is a semantic question rather than a regexp
one. It plausibly should: the interval happened, whoever it belongs to, and
writing it twice is wrong under either reading. That is a decision this heading
now owes.

*Not yet reproduced end to end on the corpus.* The user's own =10:16--10:21=
flip could not be located — no =2026-09-04= active range on disk beyond one
=review pass (buffer buried)= annotation at =11:28--11:30=, and both org buffers
report unmodified — so where the edit was made is unknown and whether the
re-offer came from *this* mechanism or simply from the review buffer having no
toggle to persist is unsettled. The batch measurement above stands on its own
either way.

*Correction, 2026-09-04, and it retires most of this heading. **The kind toggle
already exists.***

The body above says the review buffer "has =e= for editing an interval's *times*
but nothing for its *kind*". That stopped being true on **2026-08-24**, in
response to the very incident it describes — =[16:00]--[16:00]= edited to
=<16:00>--<16:31>=, times kept, assertion dropped.
=claude-code-ide-org-review-edit-interval= now reads the bracket style back:

: (plist-put item :active (and (string-prefix-p "<" ...) (string-prefix-p "<" ...)))

and =--review-format-annotation= honours it, choosing =<%Y-%m-%d %a %H:%M>= over
the inactive form. So typing active brackets at =e='s prompt *is* the toggle,
and it reaches the file. What remains of this heading is a **keybinding** — one
keystroke instead of retyping two timestamps — which is a convenience, not the
missing capability the title claims.

*Why the user's toggle nevertheless vanished, 2026-09-04.* =:active= lives on
the in-memory item plist, and the review buffer rebuilds every item from the
queue each time it opens. An edit that is not **applied** in the same sitting is
gone — and that is true of an =e= time correction exactly as much as of a
bracket kind, so it is not specific to active at all. The queue records agent
events; a human's judgement *about* an event has nowhere to live until apply
writes it. =8d0716fe='s =g= guard exists because that state is losable, but it
only guards within a session.

*So the answer to "is that expected" is yes*, for a reason that is about
persistence rather than about the toggle.

*And my previous note on this heading was wrong in its framing.* The
active-CLOCK measurement stands — =--recorded-intervals-at-point= genuinely
returns nil for =CLOCK: <...>--<...>= — but that path is a human hand-editing a
**CLOCK line in the file**, not the review flow, which writes an *annotation*
and consults CLOCK lines for idempotency. Two different things, and I ran them
together.

*Correction 2026-09-14: the flip **is** on disk, and this body's "could not be
located" is wrong.* It reads =<2026-09-04 Fri 10:16>--<2026-09-04 Fri 10:21>
read the testdata report; planned the wrap-up sequence=, in the meta-work
datetree's 2026-09-04 day node — not under =Review attention=, which is where
the earlier search's one active =2026-09-04= hit (=review pass (buffer buried)=
at =11:28--11:30=) came from. So the search looked in the right file and
reported the wrong conclusion; the interval had been there the whole time.

*And locating it settles the question this heading left open, in the unwelcome
direction.* The flip has **no =CLOCK:= line at all** — the day node's clocks run
=[10:14]--[10:16]= and then jump to =[10:23]--[10:24]=. So the act of asserting
those five minutes as the human's own attention recorded *zero* minutes: the
annotation carries the claim, and =org-clock-sum= counts nothing. It is one of
18 such annotations totalling 99 minutes, measured on
[[id:2deb090f-593d-40e3-9044-fda318d9b6de][2deb090f]].

*Which sharpens this heading's scope again.* The keystroke it asks for would
make flipping cheap — and on this evidence, cheaply producing an assertion that
no report can see is not obviously an improvement. Bind the key **after**
=2deb090f=, not before: the flip needs to write minutes before it is worth
making easy.

*Found because the user asked whether the =Review attention= / =Review and
planning= audit had accounted for manually flipped intervals.* It had not,
explicitly — the audit's conclusion held regardless, since it keys on the
annotation *label* rather than the notation, and a flip changes brackets without
touching labels. But the question was the right one to ask and it found this.

*2026-09-16: this heading's own precondition is now met, and =c= satisfies most
of what remained — but not all of it, so it stays open.*

**Its instruction was "bind the key *after* =2deb090f=, not before: the flip
needs to write minutes before it is worth making easy."**
[[id:2deb090f-593d-40e3-9044-fda318d9b6de][2deb090f]] is now =DONE=, and the key
it shipped — =c=, *claim envelope* — does **both** halves in one act: it clears
=:suggested= so apply writes the envelope as minutes, and it sets =:active= so
the annotation renders =<...>--<...>=. The objection this heading raised against
its own request — that cheaply producing an assertion no report can see is not
an improvement — no longer applies, because the assertion now carries minutes.

*It answers all three of the design questions left open above, for the case it
covers.* What the flip **means**: this envelope was the human's attention, which
is why the same key writes the minutes. What it **applies to**: one item. Which
**key**: =c=, which was free; =t= and =A= remain so.

**What it does not cover, and this is the whole remainder.** =c= refuses a span
that is not =:suggested= and one of zero width. So an interval with *runs* —
where some agent work is genuinely recorded — still has no keystroke that says
"and this window was also mine". Flipping that is still typing two timestamps at
=e='s prompt.

*Whether that case is even wanted is now the open question, and it is narrower
and better than the one this heading started with.* For a span with runs the
CLOCK lines are written from the runs regardless, so a flip there changes the
annotation and nothing else — which is the "assertion no report can see" this
body already argued against. On that reasoning the remainder may deserve
**declining** rather than building. Left for the user rather than decided here,
since the original request was theirs.

*And the framing the user offered is right: these were two sides of one coin.*
=2deb090f= was "the minutes are not recorded"; this was "the notation does not
say whose they were". This body reached that conclusion independently when it
ordered itself after =2deb090f=. One key now mints both.

*The user, 2026-09-16, comparing directly against the day this heading was
created:* "my expectation is that I will rarely if ever reach for =e=. the
allocation of time feels so much tighter than it did on 2026-08-26 … when
=01849bef= was created, that is."

*That is testimony about the thing this heading was opened over*, so it is worth
recording rather than treating as a pleasantry. On 2026-08-26 the complaint was
two-part: flipping to active was tedious **and** it was being *forgotten* —
"forgotten to represent my attention as active spans". This body argued a
keystroke answers the first and not the second, since "an omission caused by
having to remember is not repaired by making the remembering cheaper."

**=c= answers the second as well, and that is why the feel changed.** It does not
make a remembered act cheaper; it makes the act *offered* — a 0-run span is
rendered =(suggested)= with its minutes visibly uncounted, so claiming is
prompted by the line rather than recalled by the reviewer.

*Measured the same day, across the 25 items then on offer:* **3.57 h of
envelope, 2.57 h that would be recorded, 0.99 h unclaimed — and 12 of the 25
are 0-run items**, which is where almost all of that hour sits. So the gap is
now **visible and closable one keystroke at a time**, where on 2026-08-26 it was
invisible: a 0-run span wrote nothing, said nothing about writing nothing, and
could only be claimed by retyping two timestamps.

*The prediction bears directly on this heading's remainder.* What is left is a
keystroke for a span **with runs** — precisely the case where =e= is the current
answer. A user who expects never to reach for =e= is saying that case does not
arise for them in practice. Combined with the argument already above — that for
such a span the CLOCK lines come from the runs regardless, so a flip changes the
annotation and nothing else — **the remainder now has an argument from principle
and an observation from use pointing the same way: decline it.**

*Not closed here.* The prediction is a prediction; it is worth a month of use
before acting on it, and =e= remains the escape hatch for bounds that are
actually wrong, which is a different act from asserting attention.

  scope shrank but the case is now live

:END:

*Observed in the applied record 2026-08-22.*
[[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] carries =CLOCK:
[2026-08-21 Fri 14:53]--[15:42] => 0:49=, and the day node =2026-08-21 Friday=
carries four intervals -- 15:06--15:07, 15:11--15:13, 15:25--15:30, 15:34--15:35
-- every one of them *inside* that window. About nine minutes is clocked against
two headings at once.

Both items were offered in the same review buffer and both were correct in
isolation: one an assigned span, the other the suggested rendering of an
explicit =clock_in=/=clock_out= pair. Nothing checked them against each other.

*Not a rounding concern.* The second co-equal goal of this project is a
trustworthy record of where attention went, and a clocktable that sums to more
than the elapsed day is the plainest possible violation of it. A remainder-style
guard already exists for editing one item's interval; the missing check is
across items in one batch.

*Related and distinct:* [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]]
would make explicit pairs authoritative, which shrinks this problem by
construction -- a partitioned span cannot overlap the pair that partitioned it
-- but does not remove the need for the check.

*Retitled 2026-08-28, because the old title had been overtaken.* It read "A span
and an explicit clock interval can both apply, double-counting the minutes",
which names the general phenomenon — and the same-heading instance of it was
fixed by =--subtract-intervals=. The index was therefore advertising a solved
problem, and the natural reading of the title was the fixed one.

What survives is the case this body already documents and subtraction
structurally cannot reach: the two overlapping intervals live in *different*
=:LOGBOOK:= drawers. =--subtract-intervals= reads the target heading's own
drawer, so an interval on 82df2a6c and one on the day node are each correct in
their own drawer and invisible to each other. The check that is missing is
across items in one batch, not within one heading — which is what the body said
before the fix and still says.

*What to do,* in order -- the reasons are in =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=
under "Archiving", not here:

2. Consolidate every drawer

3. Archive every =DONE= *and* =CANCELLED= heading that carries an outcome

Paired with [[id:2aff1e6d-71cf-4b3f-8a3f-b3fc63d07c99][2aff1e6d]] at the same
timestamp so the two land as one sitting. =SCHEDULED= not =DEADLINE=, and =++1d=
not =+1d=: the first says the time is a suggestion, the second means a missed
day is skipped rather than banked.

*Step 1 cannot be delegated* -- apply is human-triggered by design -- which
bounds what any scheduler can do here.
[[id:aa1ba915-ee68-48df-afea-197ae60aac63][aa1ba915]] is the prompt; it can
prepare and remind, not perform.

*The existing backlog is [[id:7fbab3b3-7843-4290-94a5-d421413f2be4][7fbab3b3]],

:PROPERTIES:

- [2026-08-21 Fri 14:13]--[2026-08-21 Fri 14:24] assigned at review
:END:

The =/next-session= idiom is used but not codified. One line outside the file
mentions it -- =CLAUDE.md:126= -- and it documents the *artifact* while
explicitly denying it is a convention: "a plan, not a convention". Nothing
states when one is written, what it must contain, or what "consumed" means.

*First assertion: every item states whether it carries an unresolved decision,
and whose it is.* The 2026-08-21 prompt did this correctly for its item 5 ("the
user decides; do not pick silently") and not for item 1, where "Possible shapes,
none chosen" sat in the heading body instead -- so the executing session chose a
shape with no checkpoint. That is the gap in one sentence: the prompt planned at
the *sequencing* altitude (survey, slice, order, cut line) and not at the
*design* altitude, and only the second needs a marker.

*Evidence this is a recurring class rather than one slip.* =* Documentation=
already holds six headings of the "a convention is nowhere written down" shape:
[[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]],
[[id:9651d4c8-6887-4b0a-91d9-562c3bc49a75][9651d4c8]],
[[id:241a4c54-ed59-4780-b602-2a86efee978a][241a4c54]],
[[id:bba1088c-d8e1-49f7-bef8-b070860f5257][bba1088c]],
[[id:8707de36-93a7-4e34-b4ba-48008c7e1e3b][8707de36]] and this one.
[[id:d2a0f54c-b59a-4855-ae3d-c93a6d3aa177][d2a0f54c]] is the mechanical answer
to the class and would check this format once it exists.

*Home: a new user-scoped skill, not the org-dev skill.* org-dev's trigger is
changing this repo's own code and verifying it took effect; writing a prompt at
end-of-session is neither, so the rule would silently never load -- the same
failure CLAUDE.md names for path-scoped rules. And the idiom is org-free,
Emacs-free and repo-free, which makes it the most portable thing here.
[[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] measured that only
=~/.claude/= travels; this is its easiest first instance, a skill with *zero*
repo coupling against the org skill's measured 16 lines. The format can
additionally self-host in the prompt file's own header, which needs no trigger
at all.

*Open:* there is no end-of-session trigger to hang the writing rule on.
Candidate is firing on an explicit request to write or revise the prompt, with
the self-hosted header as the backstop.

Overlap to resolve, noted 2026-09-03: the slice NEXT (:ID: abce1850 — at most
one slice carries it, meaning "this is the slice to pick up next") occupies the
same portfolio-nomination niche as this prompt format, and neither artifact
mentions the other. Whichever mechanism lands first decides whether they are one
mechanism or two, and records the decision in the other's terms — the twin
asymmetry at the portfolio tier is otherwise invisible from inside either.
Related and deliberately deferred: one-NEXT-per-slice has no lint or report yet;
observe the days-old rule before building machinery for it.
