  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
- State "TODO"       from "MAYBE"      [2026-09-08 Tue 09:01] \\
  promoted as the doc task; design questions settled elsewhere
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/worktree-session-partitioning.md][Plan]]

Raised in conversation 2026-08-04: can separate Claude Code sessions
work on distinct TODOs simultaneously by each operating in its own
=git worktree= of a project repo? For the *code* side, yes — that's
exactly what worktrees are for, one checkout + branch per session,
no shared-file collisions. Emacs' own uniquify already disambiguates
same-basename buffers at different paths, so that part isn't even in
question.

Where it doesn't extend: =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= themselves must stay a
single canonical copy in the main checkout, not one copy per
worktree. Two failure modes rule out a per-worktree copy: (1) =:ID:=
lookups go through =org-id-locations=, a single global hash table
keyed by ID — a heading =:ID:= duplicated across two on-disk copies
(which a worktree checkout produces automatically) means whichever
copy was scanned last silently wins, with every =org_*= tool
targeting an unpredictable copy; (2) CLOCK/=:SESSIONS:= data written
into a worktree-local copy is invisible to =org_clock_report=/
=org_query=/the agenda until that branch merges, and is lost outright
if the worktree is torn down first — fragmenting exactly the
attention-tracking data the clock exists to keep whole. The single
clock living in one Emacs process reading one canonical file is the
mechanism that enforces "one attention at a time," not a limitation
to route around.

Related: [[id:337f7fb2-b9e9-4c02-82dd-d88e60df364b][Concurrent Claude Code
sessions share one global org-clock with no session identity]] already solved
the narrower "which session owns the running clock" problem this would otherwise
collide with. This entry is scoped to whether/how to formalize
worktrees-for-code as a documented pattern (e.g. in CLAUDE.md's Emacs
integration section) alongside that existing single-canonical-file constraint —
not yet decided as worth building anything for; may just be a documentation note
rather than code.

*Update 2026-08-05:* the core premise here — "the single clock is the mechanism
that enforces one attention at a time, not a limitation to route around" — is
exactly what's being stress-tested by real concurrent-session activity found
this same day: [[id:582cc7f4-41a2-4666-ad3f-1b76b459147e][session-resume blindly
inherits any session's last-clocked heading]],
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][Direct file writes to a live org
buffer can desync org-clock-marker]], and
[[id:d150c02e-06fe-4514-bf69-e2cc5d8df4c7][org_clock_in doesn't record clock
ownership, unlike session-resume]]. Not reopening the conclusion here — just
flagging that if/when this gets planned, it should be read against that fresh
evidence, not just the original 2026-07-30 reasoning.

*The practice arrived without the decision, 2026-09-03 — noted, but not grounds
for promotion.* This body says worktrees-for-code is "not yet decided as worth
building anything for; may just be a documentation note". Since then a worktree
has become a normal way to review a PR here: one was used this session to
establish that =bin/pre-commit-test='s failures reproduce at =8c52cd6=, and
=f8c86914= exists precisely because reviewing PR 8 from
=.claude/worktrees/pr8-review= made a convention check report the wrong cause.

Held at =MAYBE= because =f8c86914= already carries the concrete defect and is
=TODO=. Promoting this too would put the same work in the slice twice under two
framings — the duplication this project rejects elsewhere. What is left here is
the *documentation* half, and it is worth writing only once someone decides the
pattern is sanctioned rather than merely happening.

*Re-examined at =ff7ccb2d='s Step 6 (2026-09-08, at the user's ask) and
promoted — as a documentation task, which is all that remains.* The
user-global queue design was re-evaluated against a month of practice
and vindicated on three grounds: worktree sessions were exercised for
real (the PR-8 review) and their events landed in the one directory,
resolving through the one live Emacs to the one canonical tracker; the
=cwd= field (recording since 2026-09-04) answers the multi-project
interleaving that user-level hooks will bring with recorded provenance
rather than directory partitioning, normalising a worktree to its repo
via the common git dir (=f8c86914='s move); and the clock-desync trio
cited above is largely moot post-cutover, since state and clock writes
no longer touch the live buffer from any checkout — only direct file
writes (=53b0047d=) retain the risk. Per-worktree tracker copies stay
ruled out by org-id's last-scan-wins, this body's original point.

*The promoted scope is narrow*: write the worktrees-for-code pattern
and the one-canonical-tracker constraint down for a reader — README's
Known-limitations or CLAUDE.md — now that the pattern is sanctioned in
code by the worktree-aware convention checks. No partitioning
machinery; the design questions all have their own headings.


* MAYBE [1/4] Accumulate issues to report upstream to claude-code-ide.el :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Upstream
:ID:       9d6449c7-529f-49a0-b28f-015cce869715
:CREATED:  [2026-07-30 Thu 12:21]
:END:
A running collector, not a single deliverable — whenever this
project's use of =claude-code-ide.el= turns up a genuine bug or
spec-compliance gap in the package itself, it gets a subtask here
rather than being silently patched and forgotten in personal Doom
config. Filing upstream is optional/whenever; the point is not
losing track of what's been found.


** TODO MCP HTTP server responds 200 instead of 202 Accepted to notifications :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8f986c6f-7cab-42c5-9a21-01275f271c80
:CREATED:  [2026-07-30 Thu 12:21]
:END:
=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--send-empty-response= (the
id-less/notification response path, e.g. for
=notifications/initialized=) replies with HTTP =200= +
=Content-Type: text/plain=, but the MCP Streamable HTTP spec expects
=202 Accepted= for that case. Confirmed live, found while bridging
Warp to this server: Warp's own MCP client fails with "Transport
channel closed" sending =notifications/initialized= against the
unpatched server; the Python =mcp= SDK's client (used by
=mcp-proxy=) instead rejects the same response with "Unexpected
content type: text/plain". Verified fix: changing just the status
code to =202= (confirmed transiently via =emacsclient= before being
persisted as a Doom config override) resolves both — a full
=tools/list= round-trip succeeded afterward. Worked around locally in
this project's Doom config pending an upstream report/PR.


** TODO json-rpc-error is signalled but never define-error'd, so a bad request hangs the connection :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ce012ad4-87f7-435c-a2ee-25210c071865
:CREATED:  [2026-09-02 Wed 12:10]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-09 Wed 16:45]--[2026-09-09 Wed 16:46] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-09 Wed 16:45]--[2026-09-09 Wed 16:46] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-09 Wed 17:38]--[2026-09-09 Wed 17:45] =>  0:07
- [2026-09-09 Wed 17:38]--[2026-09-09 Wed 17:45] assigned at review
:END:

*Found 2026-09-02 while diagnosing
[[id:72463b68-9f08-44b2-8213-87f71298138d][72463b68]].* This is the upstream
half: our three tool declarations were the trigger, but the reason a bad request
*hangs* instead of erroring is in =claude-code-ide= itself, and it will bite any
tool anyone registers.

=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server.el= signals =json-rpc-error= in three places —
=--dispatch= for an unknown method (=-32601=), =--handle-tools-call= for an
unknown tool (=-32602=), and =--validate-args= for a missing required argument
(=-32602=) — and *never passes the symbol to =define-error=*.

An error symbol with no =error-conditions= property matches no handler. Measured
directly:

: (condition-case e (signal 'json-rpc-error (list -32602 "x")) (error "caught"))
: => *ERROR*: peculiar error: -32602, "x"

So the =(error …)= handler in =--handle-post= does not catch it, and neither
does the inner =condition-case= in =--handle-tools-call= — which the
=--validate-args= call sits outside of anyway, being evaluated in the enclosing
=let*=. The signal escapes the whole request handler, =--send-json-response= is
never reached, its =(throw 'close-connection nil)= never runs, and the HTTP
connection is neither answered nor closed. The client waits until it times out.
Emacs stays fully responsive throughout, which is what makes the symptom so hard
to place.

*The fix upstream is one form:* =(define-error 'json-rpc-error "JSON-RPC
error")=, plus a handler in =--handle-post= that renders it through
=--send-json-error= with its own code and message. Every one of the three signal
sites already carries the right JSON-RPC code, so they need no change — today
those codes are simply never delivered to anyone.

*Worth reporting because the failure is maximally unhelpful.* The three
conditions it covers are ordinary client mistakes — unknown method, unknown
tool, missing argument — and each is the kind of thing a caller could correct
instantly if told. Instead all three present identically as a hang with no
diagnostic anywhere.

Sibling of [[id:8f986c6f-7cab-42c5-9a21-01275f271c80][8f986c6f]] under this
heading: both are the HTTP server answering wrongly rather than the tools
misbehaving.

*Second symptom observed live, 2026-09-09* (folded in from the
cancelled twin =3f31b788=, captured before this heading was seen —
the body-carrying =org_outline= surfaced the sibling minutes later):
the *unknown-method* signal site (=-32601=) fired in production — the
user saw =error in process filter: peculiar error: -32601, "Method
not found"= in the minibuffer — and a controlled =curl= probe with
=logging/setLevel= reproduced it: empty response body, no JSON-RPC
error object. Two additions for the upstream report: the server's own
=initialize= reply *advertises a =logging= capability it does not
implement*, actively inviting the =logging/setLevel= request that
lands on the unhandled path; and the observed failure at this site
was silence-plus-minibuffer-noise rather than a client timeout, so
the symptom varies by site while the cause is the one missing
=define-error=.


** TODO Unknown tool arguments are dropped silently by both dispatch paths :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       bbf9fb77-f081-48c5-bb01-1b4d4f201969
:CREATED:  [2026-09-08 Tue 16:15]
:END:

The upstream half of [[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]],
ready to write up as an issue. Both dispatch paths drop a key the
schema does not declare, silently, and the measured local cost was
four heading bodies lost to invented =todo_state=/=body= arguments
that every call accepted and discarded.

*Path 1 — HTTP* (=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--validate-args=):
iterates =arg-specs= and pulls each *declared* name out of =args=; it
never iterates =args=, so an unknown key is never examined. The
asymmetry is the report's hook: a *missing required* argument already
signals =-32602=, so the refusal machinery exists — only the
unknown-key direction is unguarded, and the fix is one pass over
=args= for keys absent from =arg-specs=.

*Path 2 — WebSocket* (=claude-code-ide-mcp--handle-tools-call=,
examined 2026-09-08, answering the gating question 1cd2df62 left):
hands the whole =arguments= alist to the handler with **no validation
at all** — no unknown-key check *and* no missing-required check, so it
is strictly less guarded than the HTTP path. Whatever fix upstream
takes should cover both, and the HTTP path's =-32602= pattern is the
shape to copy.

*One measured consequence worth quoting in the issue:* a dropped
argument does not merely fail to apply — an =org_outline= call passing
invented =file= and =max_level= returned the unrestricted default
while appearing scoped, silently substituting a different answer to a
different question.

*Second occurrence, 2026-09-16/17, and it is the strongest argument this report has.* =org_capture= was called **eleven** times with =body=, an argument it does not have — the same tool and the same invented name as the five failures of 2026-08-20 that produced =1cd2df62=. Every call returned ="Captured: … (ID: …)"=, every body was discarded, and the loss was invisible from the tool's reply, =bin/lint-org=, =bin/check-conventions=, the test suite and the review queue alike. It was found by the *user reading the file* and noticing that recently filed tasks had no prose — the same way the first occurrence was found, by accident, five hours late.

*What the recurrence adds, beyond doubling the count.* =1cd2df62= closed on the finding that "the tool was not at fault… the documentation was correct, prominent, and unread", and audited the whole fourteen-tool surface clean. That diagnosis still holds — and is exactly why documentation cannot be the fix. Four weeks later, with the correct parameter (=note=) documented in the schema the caller could have read, the identical wrong name was used eleven times running. A silent no-op teaches nothing at the moment of the mistake; a =-32602= would have failed the first call and cost one correction instead of eleven bodies.

*Damage and repair.* Eleven headings landed with a title, a =:PROPERTIES:= drawer and nothing else; five of them were subsequently closed, so they became finished headings carrying a =CLOSED:= stamp, a clock and no prose at all — which is precisely what the two-call close convention exists to prevent. All eleven bodies were restored by =org_amend= from the session transcript, which is luck rather than a safety net: the same loss in a session that ended, or whose context had rolled over, would have been unrecoverable.

*For the report itself:* the asymmetry already noted — missing-required signals =-32602= while unknown-key is unguarded — now has a measured cost on both sides of a four-week gap, in the same tool, from the same failure mode, with the documentation correct throughout.

*Third occurrence, 2026-09-18 — same tool, same invented name, two calls.* =org_capture= called twice with =body=; both returned ="Captured: … (ID: …)"= and both headings landed as a title and a =:PROPERTIES:= drawer with no prose (=2cd74268=, =0b5cacf5=). The session was working *in this repository*, with this heading and its two prior occurrences already on disk. Restored by =org_amend= from the transcript, as before.

*What this occurrence adds, beyond a third tally mark.*

**The failure is self-concealing, and it took a second instance to expose the first.** =2cd74268= was captured, reported to the user as carrying a substantial body, and discussed at length over roughly two hours — its contents argued about, its open questions enumerated — while the heading was in fact empty. Nothing in that stretch touched the file in a way that would reveal it. It surfaced only when a *second* capture's =git diff --stat= read =6 insertions= where about thirty were expected. One silent loss hid indefinitely; two were needed before either was visible.

**Detection was earlier than the previous two occurrences and still accidental.** 2026-08-20 was found five hours late by chance; 2026-09-16/17 by the user reading the file. This one was found by a line count in a routine pre-commit =--stat=, minutes rather than hours later — but only because the capture happened to be followed by a diff check. A session that captures and moves on has no signal at all, and the tool's reply is actively reassuring in every case.

**Documentation was not merely available but *adjacent*.** The parameter is documented as =note= in the same =:args= list the session read — forty minutes *after* the failure, while investigating it. The earlier diagnosis that "the documentation was correct, prominent, and unread" is confirmed a third time, four weeks on, and the conclusion drawn from it holds harder: the schema being right is not a mechanism. Only a =-32602= at the first call is.

*A cost note for the report.* Across the three occurrences the count is now 5 + 11 + 2 = **eighteen discarded bodies in one tool**, every one recovered only because a transcript was still in reach. The recovery rate is 18/18 and it is entirely luck — none of it was recovered by a check, a lint, a test or the queue.


** CANCELLED Unknown MCP methods signal an undefined error symbol and answer nothing :code:
CLOSED: [2026-09-09 Wed 17:44]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       3f31b788-0204-40fb-a9c0-b2bea96f7f05
:CREATED:  [2026-09-09 Wed 17:43]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "CANCELLED"  from "TODO"       [2026-09-09 Wed 17:44] \\
  twin of ce012ad4; observations folded there
:END:

The user saw =error in process filter: peculiar error: -32601, "Method
not found"= in the minibuffer, 2026-09-09. Diagnosed in upstream
=claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server.el= and reproduced by a controlled
=curl= probe (=logging/setLevel= against the live server): the method
dispatch handles exactly =initialize=, =tools/list= and =tools/call=,
and anything else runs =(signal 'json-rpc-error (list -32601 "Method
not found"))= — but ='json-rpc-error= is never =define-error='d, so
the symbol carries no =error-conditions=, matches none of the
surrounding =condition-case= handlers (=json-parse-error=, =quit=,
=error=), escapes to the process filter as a "peculiar error", and
*the client receives no response at all* (the probe returned an empty
body where a compliant server answers with a proper -32601 error
object). Same latent shape at the two other =signal= sites (-32602
unknown tool, and line ~301).

Not module staleness: the module registers *tools*, and an unknown
tool takes the -32602 path; this is *method*-level, upstream-owned.
The likely senders are ordinary modern MCP clients — the server's own
=initialize= reply advertises a =logging= capability it does not
implement, inviting =logging/setLevel=; =ping= and
=resources/prompts= probes are also unanswered.

Upstream fix, small: =(define-error 'json-rpc-error "JSON-RPC error")=
plus a handler that turns it into the proper JSON-RPC error reply —
and either implement or stop advertising =logging=. Until then the
error is noisy but harmless: the probing client treats the silence as
an unsupported feature and the tools themselves are unaffected.

Cancelled as a twin of
[[id:ce012ad4-87f7-435c-a2ee-25210c071865][ce012ad4]], which had the
full diagnosis and fix sketch since 2026-09-02: same undefined
=json-rpc-error= symbol, different signal site and symptom. The new
observations (the =-32601= site firing live, the =logging=-capability
invitation, the curl reproduction) are folded there. Composed without
seeing the sibling; caught by the body-carrying outline the read
split shipped today — the composer-cannot-see-the-twin rule holding
exactly, and the new tooling doing the reader's catch.


* MAYBE Consider archives in org_clock_report's scope (agenda-with-archives) :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       0465c1d5-a064-4c8f-8082-9b853377ddf6
:CREATED:  [2026-07-29 Wed 12:25]
:END:
Deferred out of the tracked-files fix above: once =DONE.org= (or any
archive file) is ever reachable from
=claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files=, retrospective =org_clock_report=
calls without an =:ID:= should account for archived work's clocked
time too — completed tasks' =:LOGBOOK:= history moves into =DONE.org=
on archive, so excluding it undercounts. Not urgent now: =DONE.org=
isn't symlinked into =~/org= today, so it isn't in scope either way.

Org's clocktable machinery already has a built-in mechanism for
exactly this — the =:scope= parameter accepts ='agenda-with-archives=
(or ='file-with-archives=), which follows each agenda file's
=#+ARCHIVE:=/=org-archive-location= automatically. Prefer that over
symlinking =DONE.org= alongside =TODO.org= in =~/org= if/when this
becomes worth building — it keeps the interactive prospective agenda
and the retrospective clock-report scope independently correct
instead of coupled to the same file list.


* MAYBE Coarsen :SESSIONS: to session-level, not turn-level :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       c6b598ed-9e92-4a3a-99fb-9c5194d290e4
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 11:06]
:END:
:SESSIONS: currently inherits the same Stop/UserPromptSubmit hooks as
:LOGBOOK:, so every turn boundary writes a Paused/Resumed pair — fine
for CLOCK, whose "many short intervals instead of one long one"
granularity is intentional (see the dual-timekeeping design note), but
not what :SESSIONS: was meant to show: the *session-level* bracketing
arc, not per-prompt noise. Wanted instead: a genuine close only at
DOING → DONE with the work tested and committed, at the end of a
working day, or on an explicit signal — sketched as a command like
=/dfd <task>= ("done for the day").

*Open question:* does "end of a working day" need its own detection
(e.g. against =claude-code-ide-org-working-hours=), or is =/dfd=
always a manual call — auto-detecting day boundaries crosses into
guessing user intent, exactly the kind of call CLAUDE.md's existing
rules say only the model/user should make, not a hook.


* MAYBE Idle-based auto clock-out :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       b2ac0353-1e9a-47f7-8add-e0b37994a7af
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 08:02]
:END:
Via =org-clock-idle-time=, already built into org — just needs
enabling. Guards against a clock left running because a session
died mid-DOING without ever reaching org_clock_out.

No config.el/config-test.el changes — there's no wall-clock idle to
simulate in ERT's scratch-buffer fixtures.


* MAYBE Remote MCP access :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Tools
:ID:       5adeb6c2-afc0-4472-9529-fff48f8ba825
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 08:02]
:END:
Off-machine access to the tools server and IDE-companion. This
session already got a =claude= CLI running directly in a Warp pane
on the same machine to reach Emacs (HTTP tools server +
IDE-companion lockfile, no vterm required). The obvious next step
is the same thing from *off-machine* — phone, another computer, a
CI job — but that means opening those servers beyond localhost,
which is real attack surface: arbitrary elisp execution against
your Emacs, today gated only by "it's on localhost." Don't build
this without designing auth first — bearer token at minimum, ideally
scoped per tool.

*Risk:* every registered tool call is unsandboxed elisp execution in
your live Emacs — a bearer token alone adds no TLS, per-tool
scoping, or rate limiting. Don't just flip the host to 0.0.0.0 and
call it done.

to do rather than doing it. Time is clocked against the *day node*, which is why
the day node carries an =:ID:= and the year and month nodes above it do not --
they are org-datetree's own scaffolding, and =bin/lint-org= knows the difference
